# Niemann
<note>The name "Niemann" likely refers to an 18th-century owner or the scribe of the manuscript. The bold, decorative flourish was a common way to sign important documents or personal books during this period.</note>

<note>At the bottom of the page, there is an upside-down inscription added later, likely a shelf mark for a library or a specific cataloging date.</note>

<term>Hermetic Philosophy</term>: A tradition of esoteric knowledge and alchemy following the teachings attributed to the legendary figure Hermes Trismegistus.
<term>Tract</term>: A short treatise or essay. It was common in the early modern period to bind several different "tracts" together into a single volume to save on binding costs.
<term>Binding</term>: The physical cover and stitching of the book. The notes in the margin show that the owner paid separately for the texts and the professional binding.

<summary>This opening spread features a modern alchemical bookplate and an 18th-century ownership signature by a person named Niemann. Marginalia on the right page provide a rare glimpse into the historical cost of the book’s individual treatises and its binding.</summary>
<keywords>provenance, Niemann, alchemy, bookbinding, Hermeticism, historical currency</keywords>

### Frankfurt am Main /
# Published by the Heirs of the late Joh. David Zunner /
### and Joh. Adam Jungen / 1712.

<term>Philipp Jacob Spener</term>: (1635–1705) A prominent German theologian known as the "Father of Pietism," a movement within Lutheranism that emphasized personal holiness and individual devotion.
<term>Anfechtung</term>: A central concept in German theology describing a "spiritual trial" or "dark night of the soul" where a believer feels overwhelmed by guilt, doubt, or intrusive thoughts.
<term>Christ's Merit</term>: The theological belief that salvation is achieved through the righteous life and sacrificial death of Jesus, rather than a person's own good deeds.
<term>Frankfurt am Main</term>: A major center for printing and the book trade in the Holy Roman Empire, famous for its annual book fair.

<summary>This title page and its accompanying engraving introduce a work by Philipp Jacob Spener focused on providing pastoral comfort to those suffering from intrusive, "blasphemous" thoughts. It positions the "merit of Christ" as the only remedy for the spiritual distress (Anfechtung) felt by the believer.</summary>
<keywords>Philipp Jacob Spener, Pietism, spiritual trials, Anfechtung, pastoral care, mental health, Frankfurt am Main, Lutheranism, 18th-century printing</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Maria Juliana Bauer von Eyseneck. The previous page established the formal salutation and a pastoral blessing. <sig>A 3</sig>

<image-desc>A decorative headpiece consisting of three rows of ornamental typographic fleurons and scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>

### Well-Noble, many Honor- and Virtue-rich, especially Highly-Honored and in Christ much-beloved Lady.

That the memory of good friends should not cease with their departure from the world, but rather be preserved and continued by those left behind, is a settled matter—not only among Christians, but among all rational people who hold virtue in honor. However, regarding the manner in which such remembrance should consist, not everyone is of one mind; rather, some hold nearly erroneous opinions on the matter, such that very great abuses, dangerous to human life and especially to true godliness, have crept in.

Among the blind heathens <note>The author uses "blind heathens" to refer to non-Christian antiquity or pagan religions, common in 18th-century theological writing.</note>, from such a desire to preserve the memory of the dead,

<column-break/>

a significant portion of idolatry has arisen. The learned Jew Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE), a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher. The author attributes the "Book of Wisdom" to him, though modern scholars generally consider it anonymous.</note> testifies to this in the Book of Wisdom 14:14, 15, 16 <note>original: "Buch der Weißheit" — a book of the Biblical Apocrypha, often included in German Bibles of this era.</note>: *Idols came into the world through the vanity of men, and were devised because men have a short life. For a father, consumed with grief and pain over his son who was taken from him too early, had an image made; and he began to hold him—who was a dead man—to be a God, and established for his household a form of worship and sacrifice. Thereafter, in time, such an ungodly custom was held as law, so that one was also forced to honor images by the command of tyrants.*

In the Christian Church, such remembrance of the dead gradually gave rise to various abuses: from which, finally, in the Papacy <note>original: "Papstthum" — a term used by Protestant writers to refer to the Roman Catholic Church.</note> there arose the heavy errors of prayer for the departed, the soul-masses <note>original: "Seelmessen" — Requiem masses intended to shorten a soul's time in Purgatory.</note>, and the invocation of the saints.

<vocab>
<term>Zuschrifft</term> (Dedication): The formal opening of a book addressed to a patron.
<term>Abgötterey</term> (Idolatry): The worship of images or false gods.
<term>Anruffung der Heiligen</term> (Invocation of Saints): The practice of praying to saints for intercession, which the author (writing from a Protestant perspective) labels an "abuse."
</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that while honoring the dead is a natural human impulse, it has historically led to "idolatry" among pagans and "errors" like soul-masses in the Catholic Church. The author uses a quote from the Book of Wisdom to argue that idol worship began with excessive mourning for deceased loved ones.</summary>
<keywords>remembrance of the dead, idolatry, Book of Wisdom, Philo of Alexandria, anti-Catholic polemic, soul-masses, funeral customs, 18th-century theology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dedication to Maria Juliana Bauer von Eyseneck. The author is transitioning from discussing how pagan idolatry began with the worship of the dead to a critique of Roman Catholic practices regarding the deceased, setting the stage for a "proper" Scriptural way to remember those who have passed.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>Dedication.</header>
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

and other such things have arisen: Whether this would be the right and proper memory of the dead / that one either calls upon those for their intercession and all kinds of help / especially those of whom one was assured through ecclesiastical canonization <note>The process by which the Catholic Church declares a deceased person to be a saint.</note> that they were in glory / or on the other hand / offered intercessions to GOD for those whom one feared were still in purgatory <note>original: "fegfeuer"</note> / and did other things that would be useful for their speedier deliverance. But all such things are without the Scripture / and against the truth revealed to us in the same. For in the Scripture / we are directed in our prayer alone to GOD and our sole Advocate <note>original: "Vorsprecher"</note> Jesus Christ; and furthermore / we are instructed regarding the departed / that they either do not need our intercession / or are not capable of receiving it.

But although the memory of the dead has been defiled by some with their abuses / the matter remains in itself—namely to remember the deceased—right / just / and good / provided that it is 

<column-break/>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>Dedication.</header>
<sig>A 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: To</meta>

conducted in a manner consistent with the Scripture.

We find the following in the Scripture concerning the memory of the deceased: that it consists 1. in the proper mourning of their death. For although the departure of the blessed is neither a ruin nor harmful to them / but rather their greatest gain / so that from their side it is worthy not of mourning but of joyful congratulation / nonetheless their passing is to be mourned by us / who lose much in them / and are deprived of their help / counsel / comfort / prayer / and other things in which we previously enjoyed their company. Thus / such mourning is a part of their honorary memory <note>original: "ehrengedächtnuß"</note> / because it is a testimony that we had such people in them who were useful to us and others / and thus we recognize our loss in their removal; which would not happen if we held them for people who were not worthy of life and were useless to everyone. We find enough examples of such mourning in the Scripture / so that there is no need to set them down here.

<summary>The author critiques Catholic traditions like saintly intercession and purgatory as unscriptural, arguing instead that "proper" remembrance of the dead involves recognizing our earthly loss through mourning.</summary>
<keywords>memory of the dead, intercession of saints, purgatory, Jesus Christ as Advocate, Scriptural mourning, funeral theology</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Maria Juliana Bauer von Eyseneck. Having previously criticized superstitious practices concerning the dead, the author now outlines the proper, scriptural ways to honor the deceased: first, through mourning, and second, through the public praise of their virtues.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>Dedication.</header>
<meta>catchword: doubt-</meta>

Secondly, it belongs to such a memory / that one properly praises the virtues of the blessed deceased / and whatever good they possessed / or did for us and others / and to apply oneself to making this known / not only to those who already had knowledge of it / but also to others. The examples in the Scripture lie before our eyes. How does David there in 2 Samuel 1:18 and following / praise the bravery of King Saul / and especially of his most beloved Jonathan? And so that the memory of the same might long remain / he commanded that the children of Israel be taught such a memorial song made in their honor. Again, in Acts 14:36, 39 <note>The author likely intended Acts 9:36-39, which records the story of Tabitha.</note> those at Joppa praised the good works and alms of the deceased fellow sister Tabitha <note>original: "Tabea"</note> / and showed the tunics and the garments / which she made / while she was with them. When in 2 Chronicles 35:25 / Jeremiah lamented Josiah / and all the singing men and singing women / spoke their lamentations over Josiah / and made it a custom in Israel; there is no

<column-break/>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>Dedication.</header>
<sig>A 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: Teach-</meta>

doubt / that a significant portion of such lamentations consisted of honoring the fear of God and the virtues of the dear King Josiah / and of mourning / how much Judah had lost through his death.

3. Thus it is also a duty regarding the memory of the deceased; that when they have been godly / one should not only praise and testify to their virtues / but also present the same as a pattern for imitation both to oneself and to others; so that people / who perhaps in their life applied themselves according to Christ's teaching in Matthew 5:16 / to let their light shine before men / so that they might see their good works / (not for the sake of their own glory / ) but that they might praise their Father in heaven: may also after their death / by means of those / who keep and help preserve the memory of their virtue / be useful with their good example / and edify those left behind. Paul speaks indeed specifically of the regular preachers / where he says in Hebrews 13:7: Remember your

<summary>The author explains that honoring the dead involves praising their virtues and good deeds, citing biblical examples like David's lament for Saul and Jonathan. He argues that recording these virtues provides a "pattern for imitation" to edify the living.</summary>

<meta>catchword: dear</meta>

...teachers / who have spoken the word of God to you / observe the outcome of their way of life / and imitate their faith. <note>A reference to Hebrews 13:7.</note> But the reason is nonetheless universal: and there are those / who, although they were not called to public preaching / have nevertheless built us up through special / friendly reminders / or at the very least through their holy conduct; they are just as worthy / of having their end—now that they have closed their course in good works / and have been translated by GOD into the Church Triumphant <note>A theological term referring to the church of the redeemed in heaven, having triumphed over sin and death.</note>—observed / and of having their faith, witnessed both in life and at their departure, followed. Thus Paul again reminds his disciple Timothy of the unfeigned faith / which previously dwelt in his grandmother Lois and mother Eunice / in order to refresh him for an even more earnest following.

Next, fourthly, it also belongs to the memory of the dear departed / that / in the event they have left any of their own behind / all possible love and faithfulness be shown to them as well. This was done by David of old / who sought to immortalize the memory of his dear

<column-break/>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>Dedication.</header>
<sig>A 6</sig>
<meta>catchword: memory</meta>

dear friend Jonathan / not only with a recorded song of praise / but also by showing love to those he left behind. As we read / that in 2 Samuel 9:1 / he made inquiry / whether anyone remained of Saul’s house / to whom he might show mercy / for Jonathan’s sake: and after he heard of Mephibosheth / he gave him all the fields and inheritance of his father Saul / and additionally his sustenance at the royal table.

Regarding such duties / which we are bound by the guidance of Scripture to perform for our blessedly departed friends / I rightly remind myself / as it is also my obligation / concerning Your Noble and Virtuous Ladyship’s <note>Addressing the widow, Maria Juliana Bauer von Eyseneck.</note> most beloved late husband / the late Well-Noble and Respected Mr. **Johann Vincentz Bauern von Eyseneck** / my particularly esteemed friend / after the same was called away from this vanity <note>original: "Eitelkeit"; a common Baroque expression for the fleeting, mortal world.</note> on August 16 of the past year 1672 / according to God’s inscrutable counsel / and his soul was translated into the kingdom of Glory / the memory...

<summary>This section explains that honoring the dead involves following their godly example and showing kindness to their surviving family, citing the biblical examples of Timothy's family and David's care for Mephibosheth. The author then transitions to his specific duty to honor the memory of the recently deceased Johann Vincentz Bauer von Eyseneck.</summary>

<keywords>Hebrews 13:7, Church Triumphant, Timothy, Lois, Eunice, David, Jonathan, Mephibosheth, Johann Vincentz Bauer von Eyseneck, 1672, mourning customs, Christian duty</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>The text continues from the previous page where Spener was describing how King David honored the memory of Jonathan by showing mercy to his descendants, suggesting we should likewise honor the memory of the godly.</meta>

...memory, not to let it be neglected, but to honor it throughout my life as much as is possible for me.

1. How painful his departure was for me, I cannot express here. Yet I confess that this loss pained me less than at other times the deaths of other close and beloved relatives. I had to acknowledge that God has taken a faithful friend from me, of whose entirely sincere goodwill and most certain readiness to help—should I have needed it—I could be assured. I experienced this in deed already in the year 1660 <note>original: "Anno 1660"</note>, when we were housemates and shared a table in Geneva <note>original: "Genff"</note> and I lay gravely ill for a long time. I must value this loss much more highly than if some other harm had befallen me, because friends of this kind are not common. However, the fact that I had to see such a certain and faithful friend pass away is not everything that I mourn having lost in him.

<column-break/>

<page-num>13</page-num>

### Dedication

For just as it was more important to me that I knew him as a person who possessed a heartfelt zeal to help edify his **fellow man** <term>fellow man: Spener uses "Neben-Mensch," a term emphasizing one's neighbor or fellow human being in a Christian communal sense</term> to the best of his ability—the kind of people who should always be more pleasing to us than those who are ready to show us the greatest physical favors in our own person—so I have primarily had to mourn, and do mourn, the loss of a heartfelt instrument through his death. Through him, I hoped much more would be accomplished for the glory of the great God and the edification of our fellow man; yet I had to see such hope fall away.

I readily admit my weakness; though I cannot sufficiently recognize the high and wisest governance of God in anything, almost nothing seems more difficult to me in such matters than to comprehend how it accords with Divine goodness and wisdom that the Lord of Death and Life, who holds the lives of all people in His hands, and to preserve and...

<summary>Spener reflects on the personal and spiritual loss of a dear friend with whom he shared a formative time in Geneva, noting that the loss of such a godly "instrument" for edifying others is a difficult providence to understand.</summary>
<keywords>friendship, Geneva, illness, edification, mourning, Divine Providence, fellow man</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>The text continues from the previous page where Spener was expressing his grief over the death of a close friend and "instrument" of God, admitting his human weakness in understanding why God would take someone so useful to the Church so early.</meta>

# Dedication

is able to extend [life] as He will, He oftentimes takes away those most prematurely in whom He had placed such gifts, or kindled such zeal in them, that from their longer life the whole Church, the common good, or at least many other pious hearts could have hoped for and promised much benefit: no differently than if God Himself intended to diligently hinder that by which His name should be so much more praised—which He nevertheless wishes to be promoted by us—and thus, in our opinion, He should have allowed those to live long whom He had made fit for it. But as it remains written, **Isaiah 55:8–9** <note>original: "Esa. 55/ 8. 9."</note>, that **God’s thoughts are not our thoughts, and His ways are not our ways; but as much as the heavens are higher than the earth, so also His ways are higher than our ways, and His thoughts than our thoughts:** Thus I must also rightly—though I do not understand by myself how to reconcile myself to such wonderful and contradictory governance of God—

<column-break/>

# Dedication

—lay my hand upon my mouth and recognize in quiet humility that what I imagined of His ways was pure foolishness: and where it seemed to me that God should have acted otherwise, it was a wisdom incomprehensible to me: indeed, the Lord often wishes to show us, by withdrawing those who seem entirely necessary to us for carrying out His work, that He requires no human, but is able to bring His will to fruition Himself. In the meantime, it is not contrary to His order to mourn those whom we regard as useful **instruments of Divine Honor** <term>instruments of Divine Honor: Spener uses the Latin-German hybrid "instrumenta Göttlicher Ehre." In Pietist thought, a person is a tool or "instrument" through which God acts in the world.</term>, and in their withdrawal to lament the loss lying before our eyes: indeed, such [mourning] is a piece of the **honorable memory** <term>honorable memory: Spener uses "ehrengedächtnüß," a term for a memorial or the act of keeping someone's legacy alive as a moral example for others.</term> to which we are bound to them.

Secondly, the aforementioned [friend], your noble, virtuous, and blessed heart, has well deserved that—just as by others, so also by me—the good that God placed in him be praised, and that in such praise his memory be both preserved and passed on.

<summary>Spener reflects on the theological difficulty of why God takes gifted people from the world prematurely, concluding that human reason cannot grasp God's higher wisdom. He argues that while we must submit to God's will, it is still right and necessary to mourn these "instruments of God" and preserve their memory.</summary>
<keywords>God's providence, Isaiah 55, mourning, divine wisdom, memorial, Pietism, instruments of God, theological reflection</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>The text continues from the previous page where Spener expressed his humble submission to God's will after the death of a promising young man, acknowledging that God's wisdom often removes those who seem most necessary to human eyes.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
# Dedication
<meta>catchword: Therefore</meta>

As for his other qualities—how he made himself capable through his studies at universities and his travels through Germany, France, and Italy to eventually perform useful services for the common wealth in worldly affairs, should God have been so pleased—others may speak and boast of those things. I recognized in him a higher virtue, the glory of which overshadows the ornamentation of the others, and therefore I alone must make mention of it here. This virtue is true, **unfeigned godliness** <term>unfeigned godliness: "ungefärbte Gottseligkeit," referring to a sincere, inward devotion that is not just for outward show.</term>. Indeed, I can and do give this testimony: that I found in him an uncommon light of the knowledge of God. In this, the true marks were found that showed it was not merely the **letter**, but the **Spirit** <note>original: "nicht buchstaben / sondern Geist"</note> within him. This was because he applied himself with heartfelt zeal to serve his God according to the measure of grace given to him, and was diligent to use the **talent** <note>original: "pfund," referencing the "pounds" or "talents" in the biblical parables of Christ (Luke 19), meaning the gifts God entrusts to a person.</note> he received solely for the honor of the Giver. Thus, his faith proved itself powerful through its fruits.

<column-break/>

<page-num>17</page-num>
# Dedication
<meta>catchword: future</meta>

Therefore, there arose in him a love for the Divine Word, not only to hear it publicly with devotion, but to further reflect upon what he had heard at home; to read the Holy Scriptures and godly books (among which he especially favored the late **Johann Arndt's** <term>Johann Arndt: (1555–1621), a highly influential Lutheran theologian whose book "True Christianity" emphasized personal devotion over mere doctrine.</term> spiritual books on **True Christianity**) to contemplate what he read, and thus to grow more and more in true spiritual knowledge.

From this it further arose—because he knew that our Christianity consists not only in the **light of the understanding**, but also in the transformation of the will and the entire life—that he also applied himself to lead a life worthy of the calling to which he was called. He was so diligent that, especially as often as he intended to partake of **Holy Communion**, he examined his conscience to recognize that in which he was still primarily lacking, so that he might then more easily improve himself and avoid those **stumbling blocks** <note>original: "steinen des anstoßes"</note> he had discovered in the future—

<summary>Spener praises the deceased's "unfeigned godliness," noting that his faith was a matter of the Spirit rather than just intellectual learning. He describes the young man's devotion to Scripture, his admiration for Johann Arndt, and his practice of rigorous self-examination before taking Communion.</summary>
<keywords>piety, Johann Arndt, True Christianity, Holy Communion, spiritual growth, university studies, transformation of the will</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues Philip Jakob Spener’s description of the deceased young man’s character. The previous page discussed how the young man studied the Bible and the works of Johann Arndt at home, moving beyond mere academic study to a heartfelt application of faith.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>
# Dedication
<header>Dedication</header>

...to guard himself all the more diligently: so that he often mourned the former vanity of his lustful youth, which was still entangled in the world. From those memories, he always drew a motivation to make an even more earnest effort to replace what he had previously neglected. Thus, for several years, he showed in everything he did or did not do that he did not consider **Christianity** and its practice to be a secondary matter, as the great masses do, but rather the only necessary **Main Work** <term>Main Work: "Hauptwerck," a central concept in Pietism where one's primary vocation is their spiritual life and relationship with God.</term> and the very purpose of his life.

No less should be attributed to this cause: that he not only strove to increase his own progress in what is good, but also was not ashamed to diligently perform his office as a **house-priest** <term>house-priest: "haußpriester," referring to the "Priesthood of All Believers," where the head of a household is responsible for the spiritual guidance and "pastoral" care of those living under his roof.</term> toward those in his household. Furthermore, whenever he found an opportunity to edify his neighbor, he did not ignore it. Instead, he rejoiced to be part of spiritual, edifying conversations and to speak with others about the **one thing necessary** <note>original: "einigen nothwendigen," a reference to Luke 10:42, where Jesus tells Martha that only one thing is truly necessary: listening to His word.</note> (instead of the fact that most

<meta>catchword: most</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>19</page-num>
# Dedication
<header>Dedication</header>

most talk heard in general is about vain or even useless things). Therefore, whenever he was among good friends, he himself sought out opportunities to bring up such topics from which a **godly conversation** might arise. However, he often complained from the heart that usually, as soon as such subjects were introduced, they were interrupted by others who are **worldly-minded** with all sorts of completely unrelated discourse. He lamented that if a tiny spark were lit, so to speak, it was as if it were extinguished by this "spittle" <note>Spener uses "speichel" (spittle) as a vivid, slightly repulsed metaphor for how idle, worldly chatter kills the spirit of a holy conversation.</note>. This is the state to which Christianity has sadly declined: it is almost considered a shame among us Christians—and for anyone who is not a preacher, it is considered too **preachy** <term>preachy: "pfäffisch," literally "priestly" or "popish," used here as a derogatory slur against laypeople who took their faith seriously enough to talk about it openly.</term>—to speak of godly things. It is as if God had given us our tongues for nothing but earthly and worldly matters. Because of this, the late gentleman was at various times treated almost contemptuously by some and had to endure such remarks,

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

From this also flows a third point: that I myself shall frequently set before me as an example to follow this good which was placed in him by the Giver of all good things and worked through him. # To the Christian Reader

**I wish from the GOD of grace and all comfort the powerful grace of the Holy Spirit in our Savior Jesus Christ, so that through His victory we may overcome all temptations and trials of the devil, the world, and our own flesh!**

About ten years ago, when I was living in Strasbourg, I had the opportunity to become acquainted with a Christian person. Out of the great trust she placed in me, she frequently complained of how she was often plagued—against her will and even when she intended to perform the holiest of exercises—by the evil enemy <note>original: "bösen feind," a common reference to Satan</note> with entirely evil and blasphemous thoughts. Through this, her Christianity was made difficult for her; indeed, her whole life was made quite bitter, and she painfully lamented her misery. I endeavored, according to the grace that God had granted,

<column-break/>

<header>Preface.</header>
<page-num>31</page-num>
<sig>B 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: so</meta>

granted, to speak comfort to her in such **temptation** <term>temptation: translated from "Anfechtung," a term describing an intense spiritual trial or crisis of faith where one feels attacked by doubt or the devil.</term>. Because I then noticed that such comfort was received reasonably well, but that the spoken word did not remain so firmly in her memory, I set my simple thoughts on such matters down on paper for the benefit of said person, thinking little of any further use for such an essay.

However, since I have recognized through experience in my current office here that such temptation is much more common than one might think, and that among my beloved listeners there is no lack of those who stand in need of instruction and comfort in this matter, I looked for an opportunity to address such an important and widely necessary subject in a public assembly in my sermon. My goal was to provide guidance to those afflicted people who could not come to terms with the matter and—out of a fear and shyness almost common to such people—dared not speak of it to anyone, but instead secretly tortured themselves; so that they might either

<summary>Spener introduces his treatise on spiritual trials, explaining that it began as private advice for a woman in Strasbourg plagued by blasphemous thoughts. He decided to publish these thoughts after realizing many of his current congregants suffer from similar secret spiritual anxieties.</summary>

<keywords>Strasbourg, spiritual temptation, blasphemy, pastoral care, comfort, sermon, inner peace</keywords>

Since it happened that in the year 1670, in the Gospel lesson for Reminiscere Sunday <note>The second Sunday of Lent, named for the Latin Introit *Reminiscere* ("Remember your mercies").</note>, it fell to me in the regular order to treat the words of Matthew 15:22, **"My daughter is severely tormented by a demon,"** <note>original: "Meine tochter wird vom teuffel übel geplaget"</note> I made the resolution to take advantage of this opportunity. Through Divine grace, I intended to simply address the subject of this temptation involving evil, blasphemous thoughts. For this purpose, I condensed the essay I had previously written in Strasbourg, as mentioned before, and presented it to the Christian congregation in such a sermon. However, the material concerning the four Questions <note>Spener structured his teaching around four specific theological or pastoral inquiries regarding spiritual doubt.</note> was far too rich and could not be completed in a single sermon. Therefore, the first time, I limited myself to two Questions. On the following Sunday, Oculi <note>The third Sunday of Lent, named for the Latin Introit *Oculi mei* ("My eyes are ever toward the Lord").</note>, I departed from my usual order and, from the regular

<column-break/>

<page-num>33</page-num>

<sig>B 6</sig>
<meta>catchword: place [ort]</meta>

Gospel (which is nevertheless not at all foreign to this subject), I considered with a few words how we should view and recognize the tempting enemy, as a preparation for the main matter at hand. However, I devoted most of the time to the explanation of the remaining Questions. After these sermons were delivered, I learned that, through the blessing and assistance of the most gracious God, the work performed in His name was not entirely in vain. Instead, various pious souls confessed that they were significantly edified and comforted in their cross <note>original: "Creutz"; in this context, it refers to the spiritual burdens or suffering one must carry.</note> by such instruction and comfort from God's word. For this reason, I gave thanks to the Father of Light and Giver of all good gifts for His grace, which He worked through me—a poor and inherently unfit instrument. I also gladly granted the requests of some who asked for a transcript of these sermons for their further reflection. Through this, it happened that these transcripts were shared further by good friends with others

<meta>This page continues Philip Jakob Spener’s explanation of how his sermons on "blasphemous thoughts" came to be published. # Preface.

...reached this place and was shared.

Since good friends, both here and elsewhere, who had transcribed these sermons and believed they read them with no small edification, requested that I put them into print, I felt no hesitation in complying. Therefore, I wish to publish them in the name of God. However, concerning the material itself, I thought it useful—since everything had to be condensed within the narrow limits of the sermons at the time—to insert one thing or another from my aforementioned first draft, or elsewhere where I deemed it beneficial. In this way, I have elaborated on the subject a bit further than was done in the sermons, even if not yet as worthily as it deserves.

Regarding the third—or, in the sequence, the first—sermon, it was included for this reason alone: the beginning of the second sermon referred to the beginning of such a

<column-break/>

<page-num>35</page-num>
# Preface.

first sermon <note>The German word "solcher" (such) is split across the page break; the translator has maintained the flow of the sentence.</note> in such a way that I could not easily separate the two from one another. Moreover, because it deals with a temptation <note>original: "Versuchung"</note> of a different kind, the subject matter is not so foreign to the primary focus of these other two sermons that they might not be appropriately placed together.

Finally, I wanted to append at the end the thoughts of several Christian theologians <note>original: "Theologorum"</note> of our religion regarding this same spiritual trial <term>Anfechtung: a German term for a deep spiritual crisis or "assault" where a believer feels attacked by doubt, sin, or the devil.</term> of godless thoughts. This includes the work of a famous Reformed Englishman <note>Spener likely refers to a Puritan author; the Pietists highly valued English "practical divinity" and devotional literature regarding the struggles of the soul.</note>, who explained much of this better than others. I did this with the intention that certain parts and points, which I perhaps only touched upon briefly, might be read more thoroughly in their own explanations. Furthermore, I hoped that what I have taught might be further confirmed by their agreement, while also providing additional material for the reader’s further reflection.

As for the rest, I do not present this work as if...

# Preface.

...this important matter has been perfectly executed according to its dignity; rather, I only hope that simple people will find some edifying report herein, and then guidance to further contemplate the matter in the fear of the **Lord**. But whoever desires more instruction on this will have to seek it from others; for I do not doubt that the work currently at press by the late Superintendent <note>original: "Specialis," a title for a regional church administrator in the Lutheran tradition</note> of Brackenheim, Mr. Master **Tobias Canstätter**, of blessed memory, titled **The Cloudy Fountain or Corrupt Source of the Human Heart, or Concerning Evil Thoughts** (wherein, though it does not focus so much on this specific type of trial as it does generally on the entire subject of temptations and evil thoughts), will further explain one thing or another for those who wish to read more, or at least provide occasion for further reflection. But above all, there deserves to be mentioned here **Mr. Christoph Brunckhorst's** **Christian Presentation of Spiritual Temp-** <note>The German word "anfechtungen" (spiritual trials) is split here by the page break.</note> printed in Gotha, 1663.

<column-break/>

<page-num>37</page-num>
# Preface.

**tations**: <note>This completes the title from the previous page: "Christian Presentation of Spiritual Temptations."</note> which work describes both these and other trials <term>Anfechtungen: a specific Lutheran term for intense spiritual anxiety or a "dark night of the soul" where one feels abandoned by God.</term> in such a way, and carries out everything necessary regarding them, that I must primarily direct those readers who find no satisfaction in our modest work to it. Indeed, had it come into my hands sooner—rather than when these sermons were already mostly printed—I would have almost hesitated to publish anything of my own after such a well-founded report.

Finally, I call upon the all-gracious **God** and Father in Heaven, that He would not let even this simple work go without fruit, but rather grant grace so that those who need it may learn from it how to submit themselves to the divine order and in what they may find comfort during their trials. May He also stand by all of us who stand in this struggle according to His will, so that we do not give way to our enemies, but rather, by the power of the victory which our Savior—who was tempted in every way, yet without sin <note>This is a reference to the biblical passage Hebrews 4:15.</note>

<margin>p. 1.</margin>
## The First Sermon
### From the Gospel for Invocavit Sunday. <note>Invocavit is the traditional name for the first Sunday of Lent, taken from the Latin opening of Psalm 91:15, "He shall call upon me."</note>

#### Text.
#### Matthew 4, verse 3.

**And the tempter approached him and said: If you are the Son of God, speak so that these stones may become bread.**

<note>original Greek: "And the tempter, coming to him, said: If you are the Son of God, speak, so that these stones might become loaves of bread."</note>

<column-break/>

<page-num>39</page-num>
## Introduction.
<margin>p. 2.</margin>

How dangerous human life is we may not easily recognize better than when we diligently consider how many kinds of temptations we are subject to; so that our dearest Savior, in the common prayer of the Holy Our Father <note>The Lord's Prayer</note>, did not yet consider it enough that we collectively pray in the seventh petition that God would deliver us from all evil, but deemed it necessary that we should specifically pray beforehand in the sixth petition that God lead us not into temptation.

<margin>p. 3.</margin>
This is a very necessary petition for us. However, when we are to show the understanding of this same petition in our Catechism <note>Referring to Martin Luther's Small Catechism, the standard instructional book for Lutheran faith</note>, the words were thoughtfully set down by our blessed father Luther, where it says in the explanation: God indeed tempts no one, but we pray in this prayer that God would protect and keep... <meta>catchword: us</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the preface with a doxology and begins the first sermon for the first Sunday of Lent, focusing on Christ's temptation in the wilderness. The author introduces the theme by discussing the necessity of the petition "Lead us not into temptation" from the Lord's Prayer.</summary>

<keywords>Temptation, Invocavit Sunday, Matthew 4, Lord's Prayer, Martin Luther, Small Catechism, spiritual trials</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the sixth petition of the Lord's Prayer ("Lead us not into temptation") from the perspective of Luther’s Small Catechism. The previous page ended by introducing how we should understand this petition.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>The First Sermon</header>
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

...that God would protect and preserve us, so that the devil, the world, and our flesh do not deceive us, nor lead us astray into unbelief <note>original: "mißglauben," referring to false belief or lack of faith</note>, despair, and <margin>p. 4.</margin> other great shames and vices; and though we may be attacked by them, that we may finally prevail and keep the victory.

Two things stand before our eyes in these words: 1. What God does *not* do: namely, that he does not tempt <note>In this theological context, "tempt" implies an enticement to sin, which the author argues God does not do, though He may "test" faith</note>, but rather protects and preserves us in temptation; of which we will speak somewhat more in eight days.

2. Who they actually are who tempt us: of which we shall now speak briefly.

<margin>p. 5.</margin> Thus, we name three great tempters, or great enemies, which are: the devil, the world, and our own flesh and blood.

The devil is the chief enemy and the chief tempter. All his activity consists, as it were, in 

<column-break/>

<meta>catchword: in</meta>

the tempting. For after he fell away from God, all that was good in him was ruined, so that not the slightest bit of good remains in him. Therefore, he no longer loves God, nor any of His creatures; instead, he hates God even more, and everything that is dearest to God. Because <margin>p. 6.</margin> God is praised through the obedience of mankind, and they themselves enjoy the salvation <note>original: "seligkeit," meaning eternal happiness or blessedness</note> which he has lost, his hatred and envy are so great that he begrudges such honor to God the Lord, and salvation to mankind. But because he is too weak to set himself against God, he sets himself against mankind and tries his hand at their salvation. However, he is not permitted to do anything to them by force as long as they hold fast to God; therefore, he attacks in such a way that he seeks to lead the person astray and thus draw them away from God. For where <margin>p. 7.</margin> he has achieved this, the person is in his power. And that is what we call temptation: when he tries his crafty tricks <note>original: "künste"</note> to bring the person into

But he is called "the Tempter." As to who this might be, there is no need for much questioning, for it stood clearly in the first verse <note>Referring to Matthew 4:1, which states Jesus was led into the wilderness to be tempted.</note> that He was led into the wilderness by the Holy Spirit for the purpose of being tempted by the Devil. Thus, the Devil was the Tempter. Without doubt, however—since there is not just one, but many thousands of devils—this was not one of the least of them, but presumably Beelzebub <note>A name derived from Philistine god "Baal-Zebub," used in the New Testament as another name for Satan or the prince of demons.</note>, the chief of the devils, the Prince of Darkness, the Prince of this World, as he is variously called. For because there was doubt whether this might be the Messiah, who was to destroy the kingdom of the devil—since the ultimate fate of all things was now being decided <note>original: "de summa rerum decernebatur"</note>—it was necessary that the one who was the most powerful and cunning in the hellish realm should set himself upon the Lord; he was to try his success against Him. From this, however, it can be seen that even the devils themselves, in Christ's state of humiliation <term>state of humiliation: In theology, the "status exinanitionis," the period from Christ's conception to his burial where he set aside his divine majesty to live as a humble human.</term>, did not recognize Him with certainty as the

<margin>p. 28.</margin>
<margin>p. 29.</margin>

<page-num>55</page-num>

the Son of God, but although they had many suspicions in everything, they still always found some doubt, and were primarily offended by his lowly appearance. As we sing of Christ's lowliness: **He went in a lowly form; He wanted to catch the devil.** <note>A reference to a traditional hymn reflecting the idea that Christ's human body was a "bait" to lure the devil into a trap.</note>

This also applies to the devil when, in 1 Corinthians 2:8, Paul says that the rulers of this world—and thus also their supreme Prince—did not recognize Christ; for had they recognized Him, they would not have crucified the Lord of Glory.

This shows us, on one hand, how deep this humiliation of the Son of God was—which He took upon Himself for the sake of our sins—that not only men, but even the devil himself became completely confused by Him and could not find how this could be the Son of God. In part we see how the devil, our enemy—though he is cunning, sly, and crafty—nevertheless does not know everything; indeed, he is entirely blind in spiritual matters and does not grasp the divine mysteries. Thus it also applies to him, according to Matthew 11:21 <note>The author cites verse 21, though the context refers to the sentiment in Matthew 11:25: "thou hast hid these things from the wise and prudent, and hast revealed them unto babes."</note>, that the mystery is not revealed to the wise of this world, and thus much less to the devils; rather, that he <margin>p. 32.</margin> is often against himself and does himself harm, like a senseless person who sometimes does not know what he is doing and injures himself.

# The First Sermon /
<page-num>66</page-num>
<header>The First Sermon /</header>

...[to show] that he himself should see that He is not [the Son of God], if He were not able to prove it in the manner the Devil demanded.

For he says further: **So speak, that these stones become bread.** He points Him to the stones, which lay there in abundance, or he may have even taken some in his hand, wanting to say as much.
<margin>p. 49.</margin>
Since he [the Devil] still suggests to Him that He is God's Son, yet this fits very poorly with His hunger, He should then put it to a test: surely all things are possible for God. He could—as His Baptizer <note>Referring to John the Baptist.</note> said in Matthew 3:9—raise up children for Abraham from the stones; how much more then could He make bread from them. He should then do it, so that one could see He did not suffer hunger out of necessity. Therefore he says, **speak to these stones.** He does not say He should pray to God
<margin>p. 50.</margin>
that He should let bread come from these stones, but rather, if He were God’s Son, He would only need to

<column-break/>

<page-num>67</page-num>
<header>Matt. 4 / 3.</header>
<meta>catchword: It</meta>

command it; just as the Creator needs only to speak, and so it happens as He wills. Thus he mocks the Lord to a certain extent, that He claimed to be God's Son and yet could not speak with such power.

Here the Devil held it to be completely settled that He could not be sustained in His hunger by any other means than bread, that is, through some natural food. On the other hand, this was a place where He could have no bread, unless He were to make bread through a miracle.
<margin>p. 51.</margin>
Therefore it came down to this: either He must perform a miracle now, or die of hunger and admit that He has deceived Himself and others until now, since He has passed Himself off as God's Son.

Here someone might think: would it then have been so entirely absurd for the Lord to have performed a miracle here and made bread out of the stones? Answer: Certainly, such a thing would not have been in keeping with Christ's <term>office</term> <note>In traditional theology, the "office of Christ" refers to His specific roles as Prophet, Priest, and King. The author argues that using His power for personal relief rather than His divine mission would be improper.</note>.

**Vocabulary:** Sermon, Devil, Son of God, Abraham, Matthew 4:3, Miracle, Hunger, Bread

<summary>This page analyzes the Devil's challenge for Christ to turn stones into bread, framing it as a mockery of Christ's divine power and an attempt to force a miracle out of physical desperation. The author argues that performing such a miracle would have been inconsistent with Christ's secondary role and mission on Earth.</summary>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological analysis of the Devil’s first temptation of Christ, focusing on why Christ refused to perform a miracle (turning stones into bread) simply to satisfy the Devil or His own physical hunger.</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>
<header>The First Sermon /</header>

<margin>p. 52.</margin>
It was indeed a part of Christ’s office <note>term: *Amt*—referring here to His divinely appointed role or ministry.</note> to perform miracles, as He indeed performed many. However, He was meant to perform all such acts in order to make His office known to people and to prepare <note>original: *disponiren*</note> and move them toward faith, but not to please the Devil whenever he might wish it. Just as He refused to yield to the curiosity <note>original: *fürwitz*</note> of Herod when he expected to see a miracle from Him [Luke 23:8], or when the Pharisees and Sadducees in Matthew 16 demanded a sign from heaven from Him, because His office did not require it. Likewise, and even more so, it was contrary to His office to perform a miracle at the Devil's request, and by doing so, effectively admit that He could not live without bread or natural food.

<margin>p. 53.</margin>
For this reason, we shall see in Christ's upcoming response how powerfully He rejects this demand. This serves to show how the Devil commonly seeks to mis-

<meta>catchword: mis-</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>69</page-num>
<header>Matt. 4 / 3.</header>
lead <note>German: *verführen*—to seduce or lead astray.</note> us whenever we are in distress, tempting us to seize unlawful means to escape that distress. With this, we conclude our examination of these words.

### Points of Teaching.

We would now have the opportunity to speak with one another about all kinds of temptations from the Devil; however, since we will have such an opportunity for several Sundays in a row, we shall divide this rich material. Over the next eight and fourteen days, we will deal with spiritual trials <note>term: *Anfechtungen*—a core Lutheran concept referring to intense spiritual crises where a person feels attacked by doubt or the Devil.</note>, where the evil enemy terrifies godly hearts with all sorts of evil thoughts and "fiery arrows" [Ephesians 6:16], which is a necessary and important lesson. This time, however, we shall stick to this alone and look at the Main Lesson: how the Devil tempts man toward mistrust. Namely, that when things go badly for us—when we are in distress, danger, or poverty—we are tempted to despair of God and let our confidence fall.

<margin>p. 54.</margin>
<margin>p. 55.</margin>

->The same<-

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological analysis of the Devil’s tactics in tempting believers, moving from the specific example of Christ in the wilderness to the general psychological and spiritual methods used to lead people into mistrust of God.</meta>

<page-num>70</page-num>
<header>The First Sermon /</header>
<meta>catchword: With</meta>

The Devil attacks this very point quite craftily, and he does not stumble blindly into it so as to immediately reveal his true intent; rather, he leads a person quite imperceptibly into such mistrust.

1. He persuades the person, or rather strengthens them in this innate, erroneous opinion: that we can best judge how God feels toward us based on how we fare in the world.

<margin>p. 56.</margin> If things go well for us, then God loves us; but if things go poorly, it is supposedly an indication that God must be our enemy and against us. Thus, he fills hearts with love and high regard for temporal things <note>term: temporal things—material or worldly possessions that exist only for a limited time, as opposed to eternal spiritual matters.</note>, so that they believe everything depends on them: that the most important thing is how we fare in external matters. In such cases, it is said: **Happy are the people who are in such a case; whose barns are full and can provide one supply after another; that there be no damage, no loss, nor complaining in the streets, Psalm 144:13, 14, 15.**

---

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>Matthew 4:3.</header>
<sig>D</sig>
<meta>catchword: to-</meta>

<margin>p. 57.</margin> With this delusion, the Devil has already gained much, and he uses it to prepare the way for his temptation.

2. He makes our distress, danger, poverty, and so forth, appear much greater and heavier than they actually are. Even when it might be possible to find a remedy <note>original: *rath*—meaning counsel, advice, or a practical way out.</note> through physical means at times, he hides this from us. We are led to see no way to escape the distress, but rather to believe that no help exists and that we must utterly perish.

3. Then, when we are in anguish, the Devil stirs the fire and makes it occur to us that this situation is nevertheless contrary to God's promise.

<margin>p. 58.</margin> He suggests that God has promised He would help us and protect us, yet we see exactly the opposite. Through this, the Devil seeks to make us doubt God's word and promise; we are meant to see that these have not been fulfilled in our case, and thus the foundation of faith is void, and we find ourselves deceived in what we had hoped for. Added to this, at times, is also—

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Devil’s tactics, transitioning from how he uses hunger to tempt the poor to how he uses the fear of scarcity to tempt the wealthy. It concludes by beginning a list of spiritual remedies against such mistrust of God.</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>->The First Sermon<-</header>
<meta>catchword: [sen] bereden (to persuade)</meta>

...with which temptation he also attacked Christ here.

Now, it is not enough that we know that the Devil has done such things at times, and perhaps still does; rather, we must recognize that we are all subject to such temptation, should God allow the evil enemy to do so. He can tempt me and you with it just as well as anyone else. For we see here that the shameless spirit did not even spare Christ or grant him a reprieve from it.
<margin>p. 70.</margin> Indeed, it is not only the poor who are subject to this temptation—those who are, as it were, hungry—but even the rich themselves. For even when they see no lack before them, the Devil often tempts them so that they are not satisfied with their provisions; they always fear they will die of hunger, even when they have enough of everything. Therefore, we will see just as many rich people as poor who let themselves be persuaded by the Devil into such mistrust and all sorts of evil practices and impermissible means:

<column-break/>

<page-num>79</page-num>
<header>->Matthew 4:3<-</header>
<sig>D 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: kest (completing "gedenkest" / you think)</meta>

<margin>p. 71.</margin> Thus they are also poor, in that despite all their great wealth, they have less "enough" than another poor man. <note>The preacher suggests that "enough" is a spiritual state; a greedy rich man who fears poverty is effectively poorer than a content person with nothing.</note> Therefore, no one is excluded who might not thus be tempted by the Devil.

Therefore, we should all prepare and arm ourselves for such temptation, so that we can meet it whenever we are afflicted by it. Now, the general remedies against such temptation are: the diligent hearing of God's Word and the salutary use of the Holy Sacraments, so that we may be strengthened in the spirit against the Devil. But above all, zealous prayer: here it is said to pray devoutly: **Lead us not into temptation, Lord, etc.** <note>original: "Führ uns HErr in versuchung nicht." This refers to the petition in the Lord's Prayer.</note>

This frequent contemplation <note>The "contemplation" refers to the previously mentioned practice of valuing spiritual treasures over temporal ones.</note> anticipates the Devil's moves against us, so that his temptation achieves nothing. For if you do not fall in love with what you have, you will be much less inclined to desire more; and should it happen that you lose what is yours, it will not trouble you too much, for you have always regarded them as things you could well do without. On the other hand, he who always entertains the thoughts that he could not live this way or that, and that it would be impossible for him <note>Meaning, impossible to live without certain luxuries or comforts.</note>, stands on such a side exposed and bare, so that Satan can dangerously tempt him according to his will.

To satisfy our hunger, we do not need many and grand dishes; nature can be content with little. Our "maggot-sack" <note>original: *madensack*. Things cannot go much worse for a person than they have accustomed themselves to. One does not need much for that.

<margin>p. 79.</margin>

Then one thinks one cannot be without that which one is used to, and because one cannot obtain it in any other way, one seeks it through some of the aforementioned unlawful ways. There, the devil has an easy victory.

<page-num>90</page-num>
<header>The Second Sermon</header>
<meta>catchword: ...ly</meta>

## Introduction.

<margin>p. 90.</margin>
Beloved in the Lord. Eight days ago, we heard how the devil, the world, and our own flesh are those who tempt us to all kinds of evil, and how we therefore pray against their temptation in the sixth petition of the Our Father <note>The Lord's Prayer</note>. However, the first words in the explanation of this sixth petition—which we left aside at that time—are just as necessary to understand and remember: when we say at the very beginning: 
<margin>p. 91.</margin>
**God indeed tempts no one.** These are words that seem quite strange, given that we frequently read in the Scriptures how God tempted this or that person. Indeed, even our prayer would be poorly formed if God, plainly speaking, tempted no one and had nothing at all to do with temptation. Therefore, we have

---

<page-num>91</page-num>
<header>Matthew 15:22</header>
<meta>catchword: under-</meta>

briefly to keep in mind: I. How God does not tempt. II. How He does tempt.

<margin>p. 92.</margin>
I. God does not tempt toward evil or toward an evil end. And that is what we mean by: God indeed tempts no one. This is taken from James 1:13 <note>original: *Jac. 1 / 13*</note>, where it is very finely added: God is not a tempter to evil; He tempts no one. He sends us no such temptations that consist of sin or something wicked, such that He Himself would awaken them and instill them in us. This is exactly what the Devil, the world, and our flesh do; their temptations consist precisely in this: that they stir up evil thoughts within us, and from those, evil words and works. On the other hand, those 
<margin>p. 93.</margin>
temptations which God sends are rather exercises of our faith and remain good in themselves. Yes, even when God allows the evil enemy, the world, and our flesh to tempt us toward evil, and sometimes already...

<summary>This page begins a sermon on the nature of temptation, clarifying the theological distinction between God testing a believer's faith and the Devil tempting a person to sin. It reconciles the phrase "God tempts no one" from the Small Catechism with biblical accounts of God testing individuals.</summary>
<keywords>temptation, Lord's Prayer, Sixth Petition, James 1:13, testing of faith, Devil, flesh, world, theology</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the Second Sermon, further clarifying the distinction between how God "tempts" versus how the devil tempts, focusing on the biblical examples of Abraham and the Canaanite woman.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>
<header>The Second Sermon</header>

...stir up various evil desires within us until we, through Divine grace <note>original: *Göttliche gnade*</note>, dampen them again; yet even this allowance on God's part, and what He does in the process, is directed solely toward a purely good end. He seeks nothing but good through it. This means that, regarding neither the nature nor the purpose, does God tempt us to evil.

<margin>p. 94.</margin> Meanwhile, we can and must II. confess that God nevertheless tempts, but in a good way and for a good purpose. (1.) There are temptations that God Himself sends, and He is truly the one who tempts in that manner. For example, when He—to test the obedience of Abraham—commanded him something difficult: namely, to slaughter his son Isaac. This was in itself a good temptation and also intended for a good end, as it also achieved much good in Abraham, making his faith known as well as heartily strengthening it. And we have a similar example in our Gospel lesson <note>The "Gospel" here refers to the assigned Bible reading for that specific Sunday in the church calendar.</note> regarding the

<margin>p. 95.</margin>

<column-break/>

<page-num>93</page-num>
<header>Matthew 15:22</header>

the Canaanite Woman <note>original: *Cananäischen Weib*; a reference to the story in Matthew 15 where a woman asks Jesus to heal her daughter.</note>, when the Lord did not immediately grant her request, but instead let her call and cry out for a long time. This was not because He wanted to lead her into doubt, but rather to strengthen her, to exercise her, and to make the power of her faith known to others for her great honor—just as He finally said to her: **"O woman, great is your faith!"**

<margin>p. 96.</margin> This kind of temptation—where God sometimes leaves us for a while without light, without comfort, without a taste of His heavenly grace, and does not answer our prayer for a long time—is quite common. Yet, as it is a Divine temptation, it is also good and useful for us. (2.) There are other temptations that are indeed evil in themselves, can cause harm, and happen for an evil purpose—namely, the temptations of the Devil <note>original: *Teuffel*</note>, the world, and the flesh, which we considered eight days ago. Nevertheless, we can also attribute these in a certain way and to a certain extent to God the Lord, in so far as He certainly does not cause them, nor the Devil, the world

<margin>p. 97.</margin>

<summary>This page explains that while God never tempts people to do evil, He does use "temptations" as trials—such as those experienced by Abraham and the Canaanite woman—to strengthen and prove a believer's faith.</summary>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the Second Sermon, further exploring the theological concept of "divine permission"—why a holy God allows the devil and human nature to tempt people toward sin.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>
<header>The Second Sermon</header>

the world and our flesh to do so, so that they should tempt us in such a way; for these are evil enough in themselves and willing enough to tempt us wickedly, such that they need no driving force. On the contrary, our Lord God is far too holy to ever command a single sin, or to work such temptations and sins, or to drive a single creature to do so, as that would conflict with His essential holiness; however, it is certain that He *permits* them, since without His permission, those others could not do even the slightest thing.

<margin>p. 98.</margin> This permission then also causes these same temptations—with which we are tempted toward evil—to be sometimes attributed and ascribed to Him, God; but this is to be understood only in the manner indicated. But when we say that God permits such temptations to the devil, the world, and our flesh, it is not to be understood in the sense that God lets them do as they please, and watches as if idle

<meta>catchword: idle</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>95</page-num>
<header>Matthew 15:22</header>

idle alone, watching what these evil tempters might do as they wish. Ah, no! It must not come to that. The Devil

<margin>p. 99.</margin> Matthew 8. cannot even enter into the Gadarene swine <note>original: *Gergesener schweine*; a reference to the miracle in Matthew 8:28-34 where Jesus casts demons out of a man and into a herd of pigs.</note> without the permission of Christ, much less attack His children. If in the kingdom of power <note>The physical world and the laws of nature governed by God's authority.</note> not a hair falls from our head without God's will, how much less should Satan be allowed to exercise his wantonness against us in the kingdom of grace? <note>The spiritual realm of the Church and believers who live under God's mercy.</note> Rather, 1. regarding the permission itself, it is so arranged that He does not permit such temptation every time. The Devil would not let us rest for a moment, but would tempt us without ceasing if it were up to him. But that he nevertheless

<margin>p. 100.</margin> does not tempt us at all times is to be attributed to the goodness of our heavenly Father, who does not permit it at all times, but only when He finds it to be useful for us. If God allowed this to Satan at all times, we would certainly be worn down by such temptations and would have to—

<sig>E</sig>
<meta>catchword: have to—</meta>

<summary>This passage argues that God is too holy to cause sin but "permits" temptation as a controlled tool for the believer's benefit. It emphasizes that the devil is not an independent power and can only act within the limits God sets, similar to how he needed permission to enter the Gadarene swine.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Second Sermon, further exploring the concept of "divine permission." It specifically addresses why God allows Satan to tempt believers and the strict boundaries God places on that temptation.</meta>

## The Second Sermon

we would almost collapse under the weight; but if we were entirely without temptation, we would partly become self-assured and partly grow sleepy in our Christianity. Thus, God holds Satan back when He knows <margin>p. 101.</margin> that it would not be good for us; on the other hand, where He finds that we now require such an exercise, He allows it to the Devil: yet He usually grants us rest again after a weathered struggle <note>The original term *strauß* refers to a skirmish, fight, or a difficult period of conflict.</note>.

2. It is also to be noted in such Divine permission that God does not give Satan completely free rein to do as he pleases with us in temptation. Oh no! We would soon be lost in that way. Instead, God sets him, so to speak, certain goals and limits <margin>p. 102.</margin> as to how far He will permit him, and how far He will, as it were, let out the reins. The Devil must not step beyond those limits, just as a watchdog on a chain cannot

<meta>catchword: fur-</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>97</page-num>
### Matthew 15:22

further run, bite, or attack than his chain or rope allows. As we see there in Job chapters 1 and 2, how precisely God determines how much power He grants him to attack the righteous Job, but both times with no more than measured force: thus, the Devil cannot continue with his temptation a moment longer, nor arrange it in any other way, than the Lord has permitted him. But He allows him no further than He knows will be useful to His own people. In this, He <margin>p. 103.</margin> loves us far too much to let us be tempted even for a moment longer than is necessary, or more than the strength He Himself has bestowed upon us can bear. We need not worry that God might err in this, for He well knows what we are able to carry or not. His infallible wisdom and immeasurable love are a sufficient assurance for us.

<sig>E 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: 3. Allows</meta>

<summary>This portion of the sermon explains that God limits the intensity and duration of temptation, using the metaphor of a watchdog on a chain to describe Satan's restricted power. It reassures the listener that God’s wisdom ensures no one is tested beyond their God-given strength.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Second Sermon, following the discussion of how God limits the power of the Devil, comparing him to a watchdog on a chain. The author now moves to the third and fourth points regarding God's active support for the believer during spiritual trials.</meta>

<page-num>98</page-num>
## The Second Sermon
<meta>catchword: our-</meta>

3. God also does not leave humans to fight alone against the devil.
<margin>p. 104.</margin> As the hymn says: **With our own power nothing is achieved / we would very soon be lost. The Devil is far too strong for us / and far too cunning / for us to be able to endure the fight against him. But therefore it says: The right man fights for us, chosen by God Himself; do you ask who that is? His name is Jesus Christ / the Lord of Sabaoth <note>The original Hebrew term *Zebaoth* (Sabaoth) means "of hosts" or "armies," portraying God as a commander of heavenly forces.</note> / and there is no other God / He must hold the field.** <note>The author is quoting Martin Luther’s famous hymn, "A Mighty Fortress Is Our God" (*Ein feste Burg ist unser Gott*).</note> God is always with us; He strengthens us in temptations. The stronger the devilish temptations are, the more strength He gives—even without us noticing it ourselves.
<margin>p. 105.</margin> So that where we thought we must now succumb, we perceive and feel how we are clothed anew with power from on high.

From this follows 4. that because He Himself is, as it were, the master of the combat <note>The original term *Kampffmeister* refers to a referee or a master of the lists who oversees a duel or tournament to ensure fair play and determine the winner.</note>, He does not allow Satan to do whatever he wants on his side; on our-

<column-break/>

<page-num>99</page-num>
### Matthew 15:22
<meta>catchword: Just</meta>

-our side, however, He sustains and strengthens us; thus He finally gives the struggle a happy and desired end / so that we can endure the temptation (1 Corinthians 10:13). Indeed, that we may accomplish everything well and hold the field (Ephesians 6:13).

<margin>p. 106.</margin> His believers must ultimately overcome; and just as we saw eight days ago in the Gospel <note>In the liturgical calendar, "the Gospel" refers to the specific reading for that Sunday. The previous week's reading was likely the account of Jesus being tempted in the wilderness.</note> that after his temptation was completed, the Devil had to let Christ go and the angels came and served Him—so too must the Devil finally depart from pious Christians where he has achieved nothing. They, however, have become stronger in spirit through such a struggle. This is God’s assurance from 1 Corinthians 10: **God is faithful / who does not let you be tempted beyond your ability / but ensures that the temptation reaches such an end / that you may be able to bear it.**
<margin>p. 107.</margin> Thus we see that God's permission is not at all harmful to us, but rather quite beneficial.

<meta>This page continues the sermon on Matthew 15:22, focusing on the story of the Canaanite woman whose daughter was possessed by a demon. It explores the theological concept of "divine permission"—the idea that while God does not cause evil, He allows it to occur within strictly defined limits for a greater purpose.</meta>

<page-num>100</page-num>
# The Second Sermon /

We see this very thing in our Gospel. There, the daughter of the Canaanite woman was possessed by the Devil, and thus the mother was severely tried. We do not mean to say that God sent the Devil and commanded him to do such a thing; rather, the Devil is malicious enough on his own that, wherever he can and may, he desires to do harm to everyone without being asked. Yet, <margin>p. 108.</margin> at the very least, God **permitted** it. For without such permission, he would not have been allowed to do it. Nevertheless, he was not given free reign to do whatever he wanted with this mother and daughter; instead, he had his certain **bounds**; he was allowed to plague the daughter’s body and to anguish the mother through such a distressing *spectacle* <note>The original term *spectacul* implies a visible scene of suffering intended to cause public or internal distress.</note>. He could and might do no more. Meanwhile, God secretly stood by this woman so that—even though in the midst of such misfortune <margin>p. 109.</margin> there is no lack of dangerous thoughts and doubts, and the Devil usually stokes the fire vigorously at such times—

<meta>catchword: usually /</meta>

---

<page-num>101</page-num>
### Matthew 15 / 22.
<sig>E 4</sig>

—**usually**, she nonetheless not only avoids falling into despair, but her faith even steadily increases; indeed, it grows even when Christ Himself tests her. This was certainly not the strength of the Canaanite woman herself, but **Divine power** that showed itself mighty within her. Therefore, in the end, God provides a victorious outcome; the Devil must finally depart from the daughter’s body and leave her in peace; for God's permission <margin>p. 110.</margin> had not extended any further in time. The mother, however, is so strengthened in her faith that she will be praised until the end of the world as an example of a **faithful warrior**. There is also no doubt that she and her daughter subsequently remained steadfast in Christ’s teaching and, after every other struggle, attained the final victory and crown of honor. Thus, this is also an example of how God is accustomed to behave regarding His **permission**, and how it is to be understood when we pray in the <margin>p. 111.</margin> sixth <note>The "sixth" refers to the Sixth Petition of the Lord's Prayer: "And lead us not into temptation."</note>

<meta>catchword: sixth</meta>

the sixth petition: God indeed tempts no one, but protects and preserves us in temptations. <note>This refers to the petition "Lead us not into temptation" in the Lord's Prayer.</note>

We now proceed to deal with that which particularly strikes us from our Gospel: namely, the lament and the plea of the Canaanite woman, and how she calls upon the Lord. Because we must now shorten the treatment of the text due to the important material we have before us, we will this time take only the lament, and postpone the discussion of the plea until another time.

<margin>p. 112.</margin> LORD JESUS, you who have come to destroy the works of Satan, teach us to recognize our own inability, the power of the Devil, and your almighty strength, so that we, relying on these, may confidently and victoriously resist the mighty enemy, Amen.

### Discourse.

**M**y daughter is severely plagued by the Devil. These, my beloved, are the words we have before us. In them, we have two circumstances to behold. I. The person for whom the woman pleads. II. The condition for which she pleads. <margin>p. 113.</margin> The Subject, the Affection. <note>original Latin: *Subjectum, Affectus*</note>

Regarding the person, she is called her—the Canaanite woman’s—daughter. Who this woman was, we saw a year ago; therefore, we shall not repeat it, except to make clear from this that this daughter was the daughter of a Heathen woman, and a stranger to the commonwealth of Israel. She must, however, have still been quite young, since in the Gospel of Mark she is called a <term>little daughter <note>original Greek: θυγάτριον</note></term>. Thus, this intercession came from a motherly heart and love. For the love of parents toward their children—and the fact that what they see happening to them goes deeper to the heart than it does with others—is <margin>p. 114.</margin> not

<page-num>104</page-num>
<header>The Second Sermon /</header>
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

[...is] not only a natural affection <note>original: *Affect*</note>, but is in accordance with God’s order. Parents should feel it where God strikes their children and believe that God strikes them themselves in the children. Those, however, who wish to make themselves hard and, as it were, pay no heed to how well or ill it goes with their children, may sooner achieve among the world the fame of a **Stoic** <note>The Stoics were ancient philosophers who taught that one should be indifferent to emotion and suffering; the author views this as contrary to Christian compassion.</note> and **Heathen** magnanimity—or more rightly called, stubbornness—than the praise of Christian virtue before God and pious hearts. In this way, she brings her daughter to Christ, as it were, through prayer and intercession. This is thus an example for Christian parents and, in a certain measure, an image of the Church, which also presents its children to Christ in a spiritual manner with prayer: especially where, in baptism, it presents to Christ for His grace those who, though not physically possessed by the Devil, are nonetheless spiritually in his power to a certain extent. 
<margin>p. 115.</margin> 
<margin>p. 116.</margin> At that point, the Church—and especially within it—represents the

the parents and those persons who have been asked to **lift** <note>This refers to godparents, who "lifted" the child from the baptismal font, symbolizing their responsibility for the child's spiritual upbringing.</note> such children; they take that very place, to present their children and godchildren to the Lord, the Son of David, so that He might have mercy on them and receive them into His grace.

She laments her daughter’s distress thus: *My daughter is severely plagued by the Devil.* She was possessed by the evil enemy; now, that same enemy miserably tortures and torments such people whom he possesses, as we see there in the example of Mark 9:20.
