# The Second Sermon / Matthew 15:22.
<page-num>107</page-num>

[...the] tongue lolls out, twists the neck, rolls the eyes in the head, and other such wretched *spectacles* <note>original: *spectaculs*; the author uses this to describe the disturbing physical manifestations of the girl's possession.</note>. There is now no <margin>p. 118.</margin> doubt that it would have gone no better for this daughter of the Canaanite woman. As she also says, not only "my daughter is plagued by the devil," but "she is **miserably** plagued by the devil." <term>miserably <note>original Greek: *κακῶς* (kakōs), meaning "badly," "severely," or "wretchedly."</note></term>. Thus, perhaps even more so than others. It is easy to imagine how painful it must have been for the mother to see such things daily in her child. Therefore she came rightly to the one who could help, for the Lord has come that He might destroy the works of the devil. <margin>1 John 3</margin> <margin>p. 119.</margin> And so this help belongs all the more to Christ in such misery, where the devil was plaguing this poor human being so directly.

We see from this not only how we may present our need openly and freely in prayer to God the Lord—we may and should lament to Him how and where we actually hurt and what we lack—but we also recognize from this the severity of sin, which brings us into such servitude that the devil even has power over our bodies for its sake. As a testimony of this, God allows it to happen that the devil actually possesses some people and wretchedly tortures them in their bodies. When we <margin>p. 120.</margin> then consider that we humans are nevertheless created in the image of God, and our bodies are and should be temples and dwellings of God the Lord; indeed, that our nature and our flesh are so sanctified that Christ even took it upon Himself and into the fellowship of the **Holy Trinity**; then one certainly has cause to be horrified by how gruesome sin must be in God’s sight, that for its sake He abandons those whom He otherwise honored so highly to the **Shameful Spirit** <note>A derogatory term for the Devil.</note>, <margin>p. 121.</margin> as it were, to exercise his malice upon them—yet only as far, and no further, than He permits him. Whoever otherwise thinks little of sin should consider here—

# The Second Sermon /
<page-num>108</page-num>
<header>The Second Sermon /</header>

[...and look] at this, and read of what cruelty Satan sometimes carries out on those who are possessed, a shudder will surely come over him. Especially when he considers that those upon whom God has perhaps imposed such things have not always earned such punishments before God through exceptionally coarse sins more than others. Since 

<margin>p. 122.</margin> we cannot think that this little girl here should have committed such cruel sins above others; and yet she suffers such misery here. This shows that every sin—and thus even the sin that young children possess—is already so evil in itself that it deserves, according to God’s justice, to be punished with this gruesome penalty. Therefore, He occasionally does this to some, so that all others may recognize that it is only through God’s goodness that they and their loved ones do not encounter the same.

<margin>p. 123.</margin> This should be well-considered by those who are so reckless as to commit sin and

<column-break/>

<page-num>109</page-num>
<header>Matthew 15:22.</header>

and thus do not shy away from serving the devil—indeed, often surrendering themselves to him with words and cursing, saying they want to be his, that he should "fetch them," and other such godless words. Is it not great <term>long-suffering <note>original: *langmuth*; a traditional theological term for God's patient endurance and delay of punishment.</note></term> that God does not immediately hand over such a person to physical possession and let the tongue be tormented that called upon His enemy? Yet what does not happen to everyone is still to be feared by anyone who provokes God's patience in such a way. At the very least, <term>spiritual possession <note>The author distinguishes between "leibliche besitzung" (physical possession/outward symptoms) and "geistliche besitzung" (spiritual possession), where a person's soul is controlled by sin without obvious physical signs.</note></term>—where such people continue in the devil's snares without realization and are lost because of it—is just as dangerous, or even more dangerous, than physical possession. But we must break off with diligence to come to our primary purpose now.

<page-num>110</page-num>

...now we wish to deal with <term>spiritual temptations <note>original: *geistliche anfechtungen*; a central concept in Lutheran theology referring to intense spiritual trials, crises of faith, or "attacks" of doubt and despair.</note></term> in which
<margin>p. 125.</margin> Satan sometimes plagues Christian hearts with godless thoughts, blasphemies, doubts, and other suggestions. Just as this Canaanite woman here complains on behalf of her daughter—that her daughter is being evilly plagued by the devil, namely in her body and through physical possession—so too many a pious Christian must complain that his soul is being so miserably tormented internally by the devil. This is a significant matter, which one does not always have the opportunity to speak of; therefore, the opportunity we now have
<margin>p. 126.</margin> is not to be disregarded. We know that this is a teaching that various godly hearts will need for their instruction and comfort. Although we can easily imagine that what we shall now say about it in the simplest terms will seem strange and incomprehensible to many; perhaps some will even consider it <term>offensive <note>The author likely means "offensive" in the sense of a "stumbling block" (Scandalum) to their faith or sensibilities.</note></term>. But for the sake of those who need it, we

<meta>catchword: necessary</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>111</page-num>
<header>Matthew 15:22.</header>

cannot omit what is necessary; and we are satisfied that godly minds—most of whom know something of this from their own experience, some more, some less—will, through divine grace, consider this matter with us not without edification. But we must, above all things, show exactly what we are talking about: namely, not of all kinds of temptations, which are almost innumerable, but specifically of this kind: when a sincere Christian, by God’s gracious permission, must suffer the evil enemy suggesting to him—against his will and at all sorts of times, but especially when he wishes to pray, meditate on God’s Word, go to <term>Holy Communion <note>original: *Heil. Abendmahl*; literally "Holy Evening Meal."</note></term>, or perform some holy act—godless, and sometimes even blasphemous thoughts; casting in doubts as to whether there is a God, or whether all of Christianity were nothing but folly and a human invention.
<margin>p. 127.</margin>

# The Second Sermon /
<page-num>114</page-num>
<header>The Second Sermon /</header>
<meta>catchword: But-</meta>

Just as He <note>God</note> in other matters incites no one to sin, but rather forbids and hinders sin, so it is certainly not to be thought that He would instill such thoughts—which often conflict directly with His holiness, righteousness, and truth—into any person, much less His dear children, or make them go astray and grieve them with such things. He works no evil, even if He knows how and is able to bring something good out of it afterwards.

<margin>p. 133.</margin>
Thus we further say that such thoughts do not originate from the <term>reborn soul<note>The 'reborn soul' refers to the Lutheran concept of *regeneratio*, where a person is spiritually renewed through faith and baptism and begins to resist their sinful nature.</note></term>, insofar as it is reborn. For pious souls have the greatest abhorrence for such evil thoughts of their own; it troubles them to the utmost that they cannot shake them off; indeed, they would rather suffer any other <term>cross<note>In this context, a 'cross' refers to any worldly suffering, illness, or burden borne by a Christian in imitation of Christ.</note></term> if only they could be spared such <term>spiritual trial<note>original: *anfechtung*; a crisis of faith or a sense of being under spiritual attack.</note></term>. How then should they willfully stir these thoughts up in themselves?

<column-break/>

<page-num>115</page-num>
<header>Matthew 15:22.</header>
<meta>catchword: so</meta>

Rather, 1. the primary cause is the wretched evil enemy <note>the devil</note>, who, according to the words of 1 Peter 5:8, walks about everywhere like a roaring lion and seeks whom he <margin>p. 134.</margin> may devour. He himself has fallen away from God and has thus corrupted himself in wickedness instead of the holiness originally created in him, so that all his thinking and striving is contrary to God and His holiness. Therefore, after he similarly seduced and miserably corrupted the poor human race, but it was again accepted into grace by the faithful God, he seeks—out of envy and a desire to see them damned alongside him—every opportunity to ensnare them, especially those who have already been snatched from him and stand in God’s grace. If he <margin>p. 135.</margin> has otherwise sought in other ways to lead pious Christians into evil through the world, bad examples, and all kinds of opportunities, and yet they have not allowed themselves to be drawn away from their piety, 

<summary>This section argues that distressing or blasphemous thoughts do not come from God or the believer's own "reborn" soul, but are instead direct assaults from the devil, who seeks to reclaim those who have found God's grace.</summary>

# The Second Sermon /
<page-num>116</page-num>
<header>The Second Sermon /</header>

In this way, he <note>the devil</note> seeks to bring them into his snares (though through divine grace he must fail in this); at the very least, he wants to make their Christianity sour and difficult enough for them. Therefore, these thoughts belong among the "blows of **Satan’s angel**" <note>original: *Satans Engels*; a reference to the "thorn in the flesh" mentioned in 2 Corinthians 12:7</note> 2 Cor. 12:7, <margin>p. 136.</margin> and the "cunning assaults of the devil" and "fiery arrows of the wicked one," Eph. 6:11, 16. They are "cunning" because the devil does not let it be noticed that he is the one instilling them into the person; otherwise, since the person knows the devil is his enemy and that he can expect nothing good from him, he would not be as distressed by them as he is in this way, where he thinks he alone is the cause of such thoughts. They are "cunning" because he generally observes a person’s nature, <term>temperament<note>original: *Complexion*; in the 17th century, this referred to the balance of the four humors (sanguine, phlegmatic, choleric, and melancholic) which determined a person's character.</note></term>, and how they are disposed, and directs every temptation accordingly. 

<margin>p. 137.</margin> If he has people before him whom he knows are easily seduced through provocation into committing certain sins, he makes use of them

<column-break/>

<page-num>117</page-num>
<header>Matthew 15:22.</header>

depending on how he sees them more likely to be moved toward this or that sin. But if they are people who are either naturally more inclined toward melancholy or possess a <term>scrupulous conscience<note>original: *engerem gewissen*; literally a "narrower conscience," referring to a person who is hyper-sensitive to sin and prone to excessive guilt.</note></term>, then evil thoughts must be the means he uses against them. And in that case, they are also "cunning" because he commonly watches for the time when the person is already at their most fearful in their conscience or has otherwise left themselves exposed. But they are especially cunning in this: after he has instilled the evil thoughts, he generally uses arguments to further terrify the person—arguments that we must admit are true in themselves. He places before the person the strict righteousness of God: that He cannot tolerate even the smallest sin, and therefore must necessarily have an earnest displeasure with such godless and blasphemous thoughts. He reproaches us with how we are obligated to [serve] God the <margin>p. 138.</margin>

The author emphasizes that the devil uses "cunning" by hiding his influence and using true theological concepts, like God's justice, to torment the believer.</summary>

# The Second Sermon /
<page-num>118</page-num>
<header>The Second Sermon /</header>

<margin>p. 19.</margin> to keep oneself pure in soul and body for the Lord; and that it is not enough that a person does not offend God externally, while his soul is a shameful <term>cesspool<note>original: *cloac*; Latin for sewer or drain</note></term> of such godless thoughts. Faith must be such a certain confidence that not even the slightest doubt is present; otherwise, it is truly no true faith at all, and thus all our Christianity is nothing. These are all such things that a person, as they sound in themselves, cannot deny; he must confess that all of this is in itself according to the divine word.
<margin>p. 140.</margin> Just as that which the devil cites from the Scripture in Matthew 4 <note>This refers to the Temptation of Christ, where the devil quotes Psalm 91 to Jesus</note>.

All this makes a person so much more anxious, because he recognizes that all this is God’s word, and thus does not suspect that the devil is nonetheless hidden within it. So the devil does not really give him time to recover, but immediately proceeds to the conclusion that he cannot take comfort in the grace of God. Thus he
calls

<column-break/>

<page-num>119</page-num>
<header>Matthew 15:22.</header>
<sig>F</sig>
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

calls them (as Paul does) fiery arrows, which do not only penetrate deep into the soul and cause intense pain, but also ignite it, so that they believe they already feel the hellish fire in their hearts, and are unable to extinguish such fire through their own strength.
<margin>p. 41.</margin>

But all this belongs under <term>the depths of Satan <note>original Greek: *τὰ βάθη τοῦ Σατανᾶ* (ta bathei tou Satana)</note></term>. Revelation 2:24. Which we are unable to fathom, and thus are unable to describe such cunning.

He seeks to hide himself for this reason, because he well knows that if we once recognized the bird <note>The "bird" is a metaphor for the devil, and his "song" represents the tempting or discouraging thoughts he plants in the mind.</note> and knew that it was he from whom these thoughts solely originate, we would then no longer pay so much attention to his song. Thus he always leads us to the opinion that we ourselves are the cause of such thoughts: yes, the thoughts with which he further distresses us and drives us to despair using truthful grounds from the Scripture are from the Holy Spirit; whereas it is nevertheless he,

### [that the thoughts] of the human heart are only evil continually from youth onward.

This does not contradict what we have said above: That the soul of the godly person is not actually the cause of such thoughts itself. For the Scripture shows us explicitly that there are two opposing things within man: Spirit and Flesh. Romans 7, Galatians 5. The Spirit is the good that has been worked within us by the Holy Spirit through <term>rebirth<note>original: *Wiedergeburt*; the spiritual transformation of a person through divine grace</note></term>: the flesh, however, is everything evil that we possess due to our <margin>p. 147.</margin> corruption. These two are so distinct that Paul may explicitly say in Romans 7:17: That if he does such evil, it is not he who does it, but rather the sin dwelling within him. Therefore, God must decree such things over us, through which we may see how utterly corrupt our flesh is, so that even at the time when we have the holiest intentions, we are not once able to keep ourselves from being disturbed by such godless thoughts. <margin>p. 163.</margin>

<page-num>132</page-num>
<header>The Second Sermon</header>
<meta>The catchword "mise..." from the previous page likely intended to lead into "miserable," which the author renders here as "erbärmliches" (wretched/pity-inducing).</meta>

It is indeed a wretched misery that a person should not even be master over himself, his thoughts, and his <term>imagination<note>original: *phantasie*; in the 17th century, this often referred to the internal faculty of the mind that produces images or wandering thoughts, sometimes seen as a source of temptation.</note></term>, though he was set by God as a master of the whole earth; and we never learn to understand such misery of ours better than in such <term>trials<note>original: *anfechtungen*; these are intense spiritual struggles where a believer feels attacked by doubt, sin, or the devil.</note></term>. It happens to us much like a lame, sick person who, lying in bed, imagines that he is quite healthy and could walk well. The physician cannot so <margin>p. 164.</margin> easily take this imagination from him by presenting his illness to him with many words, as he can by letting him out of bed, where he experiences for himself how his ankles fail him and the pains begin to rage even more, so that the person must fall down. There he learns in fact how sick he is. Thus, we also require such a <term>demonstration<note>original: *erweichthumb*; likely a variant of *erweisthum*, meaning a practical proof or evidence through experience.</note></term> in spiritual matters even more than in physical ones, because our sickness in spiritual things is much more hidden from us when we do not feel it in deed and in pain.

<column-break/>

<page-num>133</page-num>
<header>Matthew 15:22.</header>
<meta>catchword: it</meta>

<margin>p. 165.</margin> Thus, when we have perhaps thought to ourselves how we are nevertheless quite pious people, because God has given us this or that spiritual gift; then God lets such trials come upon us, which convince us of our sinful corruption: there our splendor lies low, and we see what we are.

Now, very much depends on such knowledge. Otherwise, the <term>Law<note>The "Law" refers to the Old Testament commandments, which according to Lutheran theology, serve to show humans their inability to be perfect on their own.</note></term> works the knowledge of sin, <note>original: Romans 3:20; "Therefore by the deeds of the law no flesh will be justified in His sight, for by the law is the knowledge of sin."</note> Romans 3:20. But our ears are sometimes quite thick and dull to hear the threats of the Law: and we always think that this or that only applies to others; thus, what we do not wish to learn from that <margin>p. 166.</margin> in the Law—namely, how evil we are—we must often first learn through such distressing experiences. This is a hearty benefit; whereas, on the contrary, in <term>Pharisaic spiritual pride<note>A reference to the Pharisees of the New Testament, used here to describe someone who is self-righteous and arrogant about their own religious performance.</note></term>, which robs God of His glory, lies the greatest danger to <term>salvation<note>original: *seligkeit*; the state of being saved or eternal blessedness.</note></term>: for how shall he seek help who thinks...

To this is added: II. that a person then learns to understand the <term>article of gracious justification<note>original: *articul von der gnädigen rechtfertigung*; the theological principle that a person is made right with God through divine grace rather than human effort.</note></term>. Specifically, that our salvation consists not in our own piety, but in God's pure and sheer mercy. Now, it is certainly true that we all confess with our mouths that we deserve nothing through our good works and are saved by pure grace; yet our heart, unnoticed, always wants to secretly persuade us of something else. # The Second Sermon #

...one truly recognizes the 
struggle of trial <note>The original term is *Anfechtungs-Kampff*. In this tradition, *Anfechtung* refers to a profound spiritual crisis or "assault" where the soul feels its sinfulness and doubts God’s grace.</note> 
only then, whether and how one believes. 
<margin>p. 180.</margin> As long as we see nothing but good—
according to our own opinion—within ourselves,

which we hold to be faith were

easily stand, and expect

<margin>p. 181.</margin> but instead must cling alone to

<column-break/>

The greater the anxiety is, 
the hotter is the prayer: the fire of

<summary>This passage describes how spiritual "trials" (Anfechtung) distinguish true faith from hypocrisy by forcing the soul to rely solely on God's mercy rather than its own perceived goodness. Furthermore, such struggles intensify one's prayer life, transforming lukewarm devotion into "fiery" and "fervent" petitions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the sermon’s discussion on how spiritual trials improve a person's faith. # The Second Sermon #

Yes, you say: "These evil thoughts surely hinder prayer." Answer: The more they hinder prayer—as it normally occurs with words or at least with orderly, successive thoughts—the more they promote the true **power-prayer** <term>Power-prayer: translated from *Kraft-gebet*, referring to a prayer that carries profound spiritual weight and efficacy, even if it lacks formal words</term> of the soul. When such a person in their anguish cannot put together a devout prayer, and yet <margin>p. 184.</margin> feels a **hellish anxiety** <note>The original term *Höllen-angst* describes the extreme spiritual terror of feeling abandoned by God or facing eternal judgment.</note> within themselves, then the yearning desire—that God might look upon him again with gracious eyes and deliver him from such spiritual misery—is the loudest cry before God, even though it is scarcely a sigh. For where we cannot pray, there the **unspeakable groans** of Romans 8:26 <note>original: "unaussprechliche seuffzen Rom. 8 / 26." A reference to the Holy Spirit interceding for the believer when they are too distressed to find words.</note> rise up, which the Holy Spirit awakens in the heart, and we, as it were, do not feel how we are praying. In this prayer there is no hypocrisy, which otherwise sometimes creeps in unnoticed. There is no word, yes, sometimes <margin>p. 185.</margin> no inward or outward breaking sigh, but a 
<meta>catchword: mere</meta>

<column-break/>

### Matthew 15:22 ###

mere heart-longing; where the depths of our heart speak with God without our feeling it. But that is the noblest prayer that can be: indeed, it is no longer a human prayer, but a divine prayer, in which God Himself does everything in us. Thus it says in Psalm 10:17: "The desire of the miserable (who cannot pray because of anguish and distress) You hear, LORD; their heart is certain that Your ear pays attention to it." And such prayer, where the anxieties of the heart speak (just as with Moses <margin>p. 186.</margin> at that time, when out of shock and fear for the people he dared not let himself be noticed) is the loudest cry in the ears of God (Exodus 14:15). <note>original: "2. B. Mos. 14 / 15." The author references the moment at the Red Sea where God asks Moses, "Why do you cry out to me?" though the text does not record Moses speaking.</note> Sometimes it also breaks out into heartfelt sighs, tears, and—if not many—at least a few powerful words. Whoever wants to read a model of such laments, sighs, and prayers, let them read the 88th Psalm, where there are nothing but heart-breaking words. But many times they do not break out so, but the struggle remains more inward.

<sig>G 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: V. Now-</meta>

<summary>This page explains that spiritual distress and "evil thoughts" do not block prayer but actually create a deeper, "power-prayer" of the heart. The author uses biblical examples like Moses and the Psalms to show that God hears the unspoken longing of a suffering soul more clearly than formal words.</summary>

# The Second Sermon #

**V.** Use such **trials** <term>Trials: translated from *Anfechtung*, a central Lutheran concept referring to spiritual crises where one feels attacked by doubt, sin, or the devil, ultimately leading to deeper faith.</term> even further, so that a person might truly learn to become humble.
<margin>p. 187.</margin> This is learned better in this school of suffering than in any other. One learns to become humble toward **God**. Oh, how a pious Christian feels ashamed before his dear heavenly Father because of these evil thoughts. Every time he lifts up his eyes toward **God**, he immediately casts them down again out of shame, as it were: that in everything by which and in which he intends to serve his **God**, he must appear so shameful and so loathsome before Him, so that he is ashamed of himself and would rather not be seen—like a child whose father had spat in their face.

<margin>p. 188. Numbers 12:14.</margin> <note>original: "4. B. Moſ. 12 / 14." This refers to the passage where God speaks of Miriam being shamed by her father to justify her seven-day exclusion from the camp.</note>
This then produces the **anxious and contrite hearts**, whose sacrifices are very pleasing to **God** the **Lord**, and much holier in His eyes than all the impressive and outward works of hypocrites, which occur without such humility.

<column-break/>

### Matthew 15:22 ###

This humility afterwards shows itself also toward one’s **neighbor**. In this state, one learns to bear with the weaknesses of one’s neighbor with patience, because one feels one's own so acutely. In contrast, a proud **Pharisee**—who views everything about himself as pure holiness and thinks he has fulfilled the Holy Ten Commandments

<margin>p. 189.</margin>
many times over—cannot help but see much in his neighbor for which he despises him and holds himself higher. Then it is very quickly said: *I thank you God, that I am not like other people.* <note>original: "ich dancke dir GOtt/ daß ich nicht bin wie andere leute." A reference to the Parable of the Pharisee and the Tax Collector in Luke 18:11.</note> In that state, one pays very close attention to all other people, even their smallest missteps and failings—not so that one might have heartfelt compassion, be grieved, and seek to help them back onto the right path, as should be the case: instead, one takes a perverse pleasure in it, even mocking the fallen (even if one sees their repentance) and rejoices that

<margin>p. 190.</margin>
through the faults of others, we can be considered holier and more pious.

<summary>This section argues that spiritual suffering (Anfechtung) is a "school" for humility, stripping away a person's pride before God and fostering empathy rather than judgment toward the failings of others.</summary>
<keywords>humility, spiritual trials, Pharisee, neighborly love, contrition, repentance, shame, Numbers 12:14, Luke 18:11</keywords>

# The Second Sermon #

## Matthew 15:22 ##

And just as such **pride** is inborn in all of us, so even godly hearts are often tempted and provoked by it, even without such a struggle. But when they recognize within themselves their secret corruption and inward sinful thoughts—which remain unknown to others—they are heartily grieved by it whenever they see the outward sins of other people, because they are thereby reminded of their own corruption.

<margin>p. 191.</margin> Like a <term>criminal</term> <term>original: "Maleficant," a Latin-derived term for a person convicted of a capital crime or an evildoer.</term> whose life has been spared and who therefore cannot exalt himself over another who stands under the same condemnation; so it is also with their spirit. They know what they would have deserved through their sinful thoughts—just as much as others have deserved through outward sins—according to God’s strict judgment. They know how greatly they violate Divine holiness, even if they have not committed the exact same sins as their neighbor. All the more so because they recognize that it is not the goodness of their own nature (since they feel its corruption within themselves), but rather God’s grace that has held them back from other sins—

<column-break/>

—held them back, it is quite impossible for them to exalt themselves over others for that reason, 
<margin>p. 192.</margin> to mock the fallen, or to <note>original: "kügeln," meaning to mock, ridicule, or treat someone's failings as a game.</note> amuse themselves with their neighbor's failings. As people who must attribute it solely to divine mercy that their own sinful thoughts are not counted against them for condemnation, they remain humble. These are the **weary and heavy-laden** whom Christ calls to Himself in Matthew 11:28, as capable students who can learn from Him how to become humble and meek.

**VI.** To this is also added that the <term>sleep of security</term> <term>original: "schlaff der sicherheit," referring to spiritual complacency or the dangerous state of feeling one is "safe" from sin and no longer needs to be on guard.</term> is thereby powerfully wiped from one's eyes. We will become 
<margin>p. 193.</margin> careful to diligently guard ourselves against all sins which we can prevent; for indeed, so many horrors lurk internally within us which we cannot entirely hinder. Such a person does not easily venture to willfully provoke his dear **GOD**, having felt what a **torment** it is when sin begins to awaken.

<summary>This page explains how spiritual trials prevent pride by making believers realize their own internal corruption, leading them to show mercy toward others' failings. It concludes by noting that these struggles wake a person from spiritual complacency and create a cautious, God-fearing life.</summary>
<keywords>humility, pride, original sin, criminal, mercy, Matthew 11:28, spiritual security, torment of sin</keywords>

# The Second Sermon #

...and the wrath of **God** over the sin sticking so deep within us reveals itself sensibly in the conscience. Thus it says in Isaiah 38:15:
> I shall walk humbly all my life because of the bitterness of my soul. <note>original: "Ich werde mich scheuen all mein lebtag vor der betrübnüß meiner Seelen." <margin>p. 194.</margin>
A person of this kind does not sin recklessly, willfully provoking **God** further like others who do not believe what an abomination sin is. Such people imagine they can shake it off as easily as they wish; they think it can be settled with a single, "**God** be merciful to me, a **sinner**," and that sin is in their own power—that they can commit it and then rid themselves of it again whenever they want. 

Or they even think that one must occasionally do evil so that **God’s** mercy might have something to forgive. And so, the conscience of [godly] people becomes increasingly tender. Because of their constant struggle against them, those sins which are not considered so heavy before **God** actually serve to prevent 
<margin>p. 195.</margin>
other heavier sins into which "secure" <note>In this context, "secure" (sichere) refers to people who are spiritually complacent or indifferent, lacking a healthy fear of God's judgment.</note> people often fall.

**VII.** Furthermore, such people are made entirely patient toward all kinds of outward crosses <note>A common metaphor for suffering, burdens, or trials sent by God.</note> that may befall them. <margin>p. 196.</margin>

Although both diseases are among the most painful, yet when they are attacked by the stone, they virtually forget their limb-ache <note>joint pain</note> because of the other even greater pain. 

Indeed, even when the limb-ache returns later, however sensitive it may be in itself, they bear it so much more patiently because they remember the other, incomparably more painful 
<margin>p. 197.</margin>
illness. So it is here also: as long as a...

# 156 The Second Sermon / Matth. 15 / 22. 157

has to struggle with <term>spiritual trials<note>original: "geistlichen anfechtungen." In Lutheran theology, this refers to intense periods of inner turmoil, doubt, or the feeling of being abandoned by God, intended to test and strengthen faith.</note></term> and therefore feels his <term>sinful corruption<note>original: "sündliche verderbnuß." The belief that human nature is fundamentally damaged and inclined toward sin as a result of the Fall.</note></term> so much more painfully; the world and all temporal things are not only very bitter to him, but as it were entirely repulsive. Therefore, he will not immerse himself in anything, but will seek his pleasure alone in his **GOD**, where it may go so well for him to sensibly enjoy His grace. But always therewith he inwardly longs for the hour when he may lay aside all such clinging evil, all such **fetters of sin** with which he is still held captive, and may enter there where he—
<margin>p. 205.</margin>
—may serve **GOD** henceforth with the purest soul and thoughts eternally. His desire stands for this, and he awaits it, whenever it pleases **GOD** to send it, with calm and truly joyful patience. Meanwhile, he rejoices in other things as if he rejoiced not, and uses the world in such a way that he does not misuse it, according to 1 Corinthians 7:30–31.

These are, in brief, several reasons from which we indeed see—

<column-break/>

—that **GOD** does not allow His own to be attacked with such temptation by chance or in vain. We shall let it rest here for now, even though
<margin>p. 206.</margin>
perhaps more reasons could be cited.

The remaining two questions, and what else belongs here, we shall save until eight days from now. <note>original: "über 8. tag." A common idiom for "a week from today."</note> <term>Your Christian Love<note>original: "E. C. L." (Eure Christliche Liebe). A traditional, affectionate way for a pastor to address his congregation.</note></term> should meanwhile reflect on the matter in devotion and read at home, primarily the 12th chapter of the 2nd Epistle to the Corinthians. Furthermore, whoever has that spiritual book, read in the dear Mr. Arndt’s *True Christianity* <note>Johann Arndt (1555–1621) was a famous theologian whose work *Vom wahren Christenthum* became one of the most influential devotional books in Protestant history.</note>, in the second book, the 52nd, 53rd, and 54th chapters, in which what belongs here is excellently explained.

We sigh to **GOD**, who
<margin>p. 207.</margin>
shows Himself in this matter primarily as a loving, but at the same time an entirely <term>hidden God<note>original: "ganz verborgenen GOtt." This refers to the concept of *Deus absconditus*—the idea that God's true plan and kindness are often hidden behind suffering or "masks" that seem contrary to His nature.</note></term>: who alone can also teach us to understand these His mysteries. May He also press what has now been said into all hearts, especially those of us who [need] such... <note>The text ends mid-sentence following the catchword "bedörf-" (need/require) from the original.</note>

The author directs the congregation to study the Bible and Johann Arndt's writings for further comfort.</summary>

## The Third Sermon
### For Oculi Sunday.
<note>Oculi Sunday is the third Sunday in Lent. Its name comes from the Latin Introit for the day: "Oculi mei semper ad Dominum" (My eyes are ever toward the Lord).</note>

Text:

## The Sunday Gospel.

Luke 11:14–28.

### Introduction.

<margin>p. 209.</margin>

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap G featuring intricate floral scrollwork and foliage.</image-desc>
**B**eloved in the Lord. Eight days ago, we took to heart a necessary subject concerning the <term>spiritual trial<note>original: "anfechtung." In this context, it refers to an intense period of inner conflict, doubt, or an assault on one's faith.</note></term> of evil and blasphemous thoughts, which the ancients used to call the <term>spirit of blasphemy<note>Latin: "Spiritum blasphemiae." A traditional theological term for obsessive, intrusive thoughts that mock or defy God, often attributed to demonic influence.</note></term>. We have, however, not been able to complete such a rich subject

<column-break/>

subject, nor even brought half of it to a close; for that reason, the rest has been postponed until now. Therefore, we wish to let this be our chief consideration this time as well. Indeed, because what remains still requires much time, in order to gain it—though it be against the rules of <term>rhetoric and method<note>The formal academic rules for organizing a speech or sermon. The preacher is explaining why he is breaking the standard structure to save time for the sake of the congregation's spiritual benefit.</note></term>, which must always yield to edification—we shall entirely omit the usual introduction (though the explanation of the sixth petition of the <term>Lord’s Prayer<note>original: "Vatter Unser." The sixth petition is "And lead us not into temptation."</note></term> given eight and fourteen days ago can serve that purpose instead); however, so that we do not bypass our **Gospel** lesson entirely, let us look at it briefly and summarily, as if only by way of preparation: namely, how from its entire content we should rightly learn to recognize our enemy, the **Devil**.

Oh **Lord Jesus**, assist us with Your Spirit, that we may rightly know him who is Your enemy and ours, so that in Your strength we may fight against him all the more bravely, Amen!

<margin>p. 211.</margin>

## Discourse.

We have the evil enemy to consider in two ways in our Gospel / 1. as an enemy whom we have to fear. 2. Whom we likewise also have no need to fear. *Formidable and not formidable.* <note>original Latin: "Formidabilem & non formidabilem."</note>

If we then look at the evil enemy, Satan, as 1. a fearsome enemy: He is to be feared ( 1. ) as a strong enemy. 

<margin>p. 212.</margin>
The Lord Christ shows us this, where He compares him to a strong armed man who guards his palace, and who has armor in which he trusts. Indeed, there is not only one, but many of them, in that one who wishes to return to his former dwelling can bring seven other spirits worse than himself with him. All of which portrays Satan to us as a strong, powerful enemy. An angel is by nature a strong, powerful spirit / and thus also the devil, as he who according to his nature

and given to him, so that he might use it against us. ### Points of Doctrine.

We do not have to search long for which main lesson to take up: we freely admit it does not flow directly from our current text. However, we have already spoken at other times of physical possession by the Devil / as well as other matters that seemed perhaps more closely related to this topic.

In this state, one should hold especially fast to the precious words of Paul in Romans 8 : 1.
> **There is no condemnation for those who are in Christ JESUS, who walk not after the flesh, but after the Spirit.**

<margin>p. 249.</margin>
Paul does not say that there is no more sin in them / which otherwise would be damnable / for the flesh is already damnable in itself ; rather, he says that sin is not damnable to them / the believers / or, as it sounds very emphatically in the Greek: 
> **There is no condemnation.**
<note>The original Greek uses the word *katakrima*, emphasizing that the judicial sentence of "guilty" has been removed, even if the presence of sin remains.</note>

->### The Third Sermon / Luke 11:14, 28.<-
### J

converse and share this matter with his father-confessor <note term="father-confessor">In the Lutheran tradition of this period, "Beichtvater" refers to a pastor who hears private confessions and offers spiritual counsel.</note> or another preacher who is knowledgeable in such affairs; so that they might be helpful to such a tempted <note term="tempted">The original German *angefochtenen* refers to someone undergoing "Anfechtung"—a deep spiritual crisis or trial of faith where one feels abandoned by God or plagued by the Devil.</note> person with good comfort from the Scripture or even from their own experience. On the other hand, everyone is bound to do this according to the ability that **GOD** provides, and must view such an opportunity as a Divine calling to come to the aid of his fellow Christian in such need. **GOD** does not leave such conversations unblessed; indeed, sometimes more is accomplished in these private talks than in entire 
<margin>p. 259.</margin>
sermons. Along with this, however, those providing counsel must handle the matter with due caution and confidentiality, treating the person as they would wish to be treated themselves if they were in the same position.

In addition to the Divine word, the holy Sacraments also belong here. First and foremost, Baptism. Not that a tempted person must be

<column-break/>

re-baptized, but rather that a tempted person should envision for himself—and let others remind him—how **God** once established a covenant with him in baptism, promising that He would deal with him not according to the strictness of the law, but according to 
<margin>p. 260.</margin> 
grace and the Gospel. Therefore, even if according to the law <note>The "Law" refers to the biblical standards that condemn sin, while the "Gospel" refers to the message of forgiveness through Christ.</note> such evil thoughts could not be paid for with anything other than hellish fire, the Gospel shows that because these too were paid for by **Christ**, **God** will not count them against His believers, so long as they remain believers. It is different, however, for those who prove their unbelief through willful godlessness and who take pleasure in and cling to such thoughts, for they have stepped out of the 
<margin>p. 261.</margin> 
covenant of grace. Indeed, since **God** has accepted him as a child—not just in this hour, but from eternity—all the sins of his entire life, and thus also these evil thoughts, are forgiven him in baptism. That is to say, the foundation for the forgiveness of sins was laid there.

->### The Third Sermon / Luke 11:14, 28.<-

which should continue throughout one's whole life: So he has also in baptism put on the robe of righteousness, **Jesus Christ**; that is, the holiness, purity, and righteousness of **Jesus Christ** have become entirely his own. And **God**, as long as he remains in Christ, will no longer look at him as he is in himself—perhaps stained with evil thoughts—but rather as he is in 
<margin>p. 262.</margin> 
Christ; that is, according to what Christ has given him. The "Old **Adam**" <note term="Old Adam">A theological term referring to the fallen, sinful nature of humanity inherited from Adam, which persists even in believers.</note> is indeed not yet entirely killed in baptism, but is so submerged that, without our will, he cannot regain full dominion over us; furthermore, because we have renounced him once and for all, his actions can no longer be attributed to us. Baptism reminds us of all this, and the tempted person should allow himself to be reminded of it. For this reason, examples exist of such persons who, in such a state of spiritual trial, 
<margin>p. 263.</margin> 
finally knew no more powerful way to lift themselves up than with their baptism, when they were remin-

<column-break/>

reminded of it, perhaps by devoutly attending a child’s baptism.

But primarily, the Holy **Supper** <note>The Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note> is the means by which the tempted should strengthen and uplift themselves. For just as therein the faithful **communicant** <note term="communicant">A person who receives the bread and wine of the Sacrament.</note> receives the body and blood of the **Lord** for the certain assurance of the forgiveness of his sins, so too are his sinful thoughts included among those very sins. And no one should allow themselves to be deterred from it by the fear that 
<margin>p. 264.</margin> 
they cannot approach with sufficient devotion because those very thoughts hinder them. For the sicker one is, the more right one has to the **medicine**. Thus, the qualities that make a worthy **communicant** are commonly found much more clearly in such tempted people—even if they cannot see it in themselves—than in others. Their repentance over their sin is so much more heartfelt, the more distressed they are that they cannot cast off such evil thoughts, and the more painfully they

Likewise, you should not let yourself be kept away from the **Holy Supper** <note>Also known as Holy Communion or the Eucharist.</note> out of fear of a lack of devotion; it will follow if you do not allow yourself to be frightened off. Thus, the prayer that seemed cold at first kindles, as it were, more and more, when one continues with it. These are the means that one must use.

<margin>p. 268.</margin>

...ness and hope you shall be strong <note>The text quotes Isaiah 30:15: "In quietness and in confidence shall be your strength," though the marginal reference in the original says Isaiah 32:15.</note>. **GOD** has not revealed to us, either in this or in any other cross <note term="cross">A "cross" refers to a specific hardship, suffering, or spiritual trial sent by God to test or refine a person.</note>, when He intends to deliver us from it; therefore, we are not to dictate to Him or set limits for Him. Just as in physical illnesses—when we have called upon **GOD** and used the medicine within His order—we must wait with patience <margin>p. 269.</margin> until the hour of help arrives: so too, when we have conducted ourselves in **GOD'S** order during this spiritual illness in the manner just indicated, and have used, so to speak, the medicine or spiritual means of help, then nothing is better than to possess the soul with patience and to include everything in this one request: **LORD**, Your will be done, not mine or that of my flesh. <column-break/>

pleased You to place me in such a state, I please You at least for this reason: that I remain in it because You have placed me there, even if my sin otherwise does not please You. <margin>p. 270.</margin> Ah, I would rather serve You in the state You have ordained for me than choose one for myself, since I do not know if that would be Your will now.

That is the best way to reconcile oneself to it—indeed, the most necessary of all ways. For just as we should gladly endure every lack, tribulation, and pain in the physical realm for **GOD'S** sake, because it is His will—a patient endurance that heartily pleases **GOD**—so we are also bound to bear these pains, which arise within us because of the suffering caused by evil thoughts and the lack of perceptible divine comfort and delightful joy. <margin>p. 271.</margin> We must bear them willingly, even if it is against our own natural will, and thus, as it were, forgo everything that could otherwise delight and satisfy us in spiritual matters.

<page-num>210</page-num>
<header>The Third Sermon</header>
<meta>catchword: -ity</meta>

[...it] can be easily proven from the most fundamental grounds of our Christianity.

<margin>p. 291.</margin> (1.) We have recently discovered that such trials <note>Original: *anfechtungen*. This refers to spiritual crises or "assaults" where a person feels plagued by doubt, despair, or intrusive thoughts.</note> are intended by the faithful **GOD** for our own good; He does not seek the destruction of His children, but rather seeks their future salvation <note>Original: *heil*. Refers to spiritual well-being or wholeness.</note> through them. In this, **GOD** does not behave as an angry Judge who has handed the person over to the devil for torment, but as a merciful Father who seeks the discipline and growth of His child through such exercise. Now, what **GOD** sends out of grace cannot rob us of His grace, but must leave us with it, and indeed strengthen us within it.

<margin>p. 292.</margin> (2.) This is provable from all those passages of Scripture which testify that our salvation <note>Original: *seligkeit*. The state of being eternally blessed or saved.</note> does not consist in our works, but in the pure and clear grace of God, of which the Epistles to the Romans, Galatians, and Ephesians are completely full. Now, if our salvation...

<column-break/>

<page-num>211</page-num>
<header>Luke 11:14, 28</header>
<meta>catchword: 1 John</meta>

...does not consist in our own holiness, then that which we lack in personal holiness does not harm our state of grace; otherwise, salvation would have to consist in that [holiness]. We are therefore not saved because we have purely holy and God-pleasing thoughts, but because **GOD** has had mercy on us poor sinners who lack such holiness. Thus, I should not judge whether I am in God’s grace or not based on whether I find sin or no sin within myself, but rather on whether I believe.
<margin>p. 293.</margin>

(3.) Because we daily have Christ, and in Him the grace-filled forgiveness of all our sins—whatever they may be named—then sinful thoughts are also included and forgiven among them. Especially because daily repentance <note>Original: *buß*. In this context, it refers to the ongoing internal posture of remorse and turning back to God, rather than a specific church ritual.</note> is present, as it heartily grieves the person that he must suffer such things and that he cannot rid himself of them.

<summary>This portion of the sermon reassures believers that spiritual trials and intrusive sinful thoughts do not negate God's grace. It emphasizes that salvation is rooted in God's mercy and the believer's faith rather than personal holiness or the absence of sin.</summary>

<page-num>216</page-num>
-> ### The Third Sermon <-

<margin>p. 301.</margin> [the] hour of those who have tasted such grace is surely esteemed so highly that all the suffering of the world is as nothing compared to it. So too is the power of the Spirit, which is strengthened through such <term>spiritual trials</term> <note>original: *anfechtung*. A central Lutheran concept describing a state of intense spiritual struggle, doubt, or feeling abandoned by God.</note>, and which is at its very strongest in those moments when they seem weakest. Taken together, as they grow in their Christianity through this, it serves as a testimony that Divine grace has shown itself much more heartily to them through these trials, rather than one having to conclude that they had lost that grace because of such an attack.

<margin>p. 302.</margin> Finally, and sixthly (6), the examples of other pious children of God belong here as well; the Devil has spared them just as little in this regard as he has spared us, yet they nevertheless remained dear children of God. Indeed, for many, this is one of the heaviest parts of their <term>trial</term>, that they believe they are alone in the world in this; for this reason, they almost dare not complain to anyone, but rather feel ashamed of it, as if it were something otherwise unheard of.

K 2

But they should know that this is not the case. Great saints of God have had to stand in these ranks.

<margin>p. 303.</margin> That Job was and remained a man pleasing to God, we see from the entire book. But how was that man tempted? The Devil did not only suggest evil, blasphemous thoughts to him, but brought him so far that he even burst out in words, wanting to be innocent and accusing God of doing him wrong—which are indeed blasphemies, coming from the **spirit of blasphemy** <note>original: *Spiritu blasphemiæ*</note>—and in his impatience, he wished for death. These were indeed heavy sins; nevertheless, God did not turn His grace away from him for that reason, but rather credited all of it to the dear man's benefit, whom He had allowed to be so severely tried.

<margin>p. 304.</margin> How far the Devil and his own flesh brought the dear **Asaph**, we read in his confession in **Psalm 73**. There, he admits that these same forces suggested this horrible blasphemy to him: that God did not pay attention to mankind, and that all righteousness... <note>Asaph’s struggle in Psalm 73 involves the "temptation" of envying the wicked and questioning if living a holy life is in vain.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the example of the Apostle Paul to Martin Luther and other theologians who have experienced spiritual trials. It emphasizes that these internal struggles do not mean one has lost God's grace, but are rather a common experience for the faithful.</meta>

<page-num>222</page-num>
<header>The Third Sermon /</header>

<margin>p. 311.</margin>
Our dear **Luther** <note>original: "Lutherus"</note> could not have written so much from the heart about spiritual trials <note>original: "anfechtungen." This refers to a deep Lutheran concept of spiritual crisis, anxiety, or "assault" by the devil on the soul.</note> if he had not been involved in them so often himself and spoken from experience, without which all knowledge of this matter is, as it were, dead.

Likewise, next to him in such a rank stand other faithful and dear teachers, who also confess that <term>trial</term> <note>original: "tentatio"; in this context, it refers to the spiritual testing or temptation a believer faces.</note> belongs to a <term>theologian</term> <note>original: "Theologo"</note>; indeed, most true followers of Christ have had to take a drink from such a cup, some more, some less. And one cannot see it on everyone's forehead whom **GOD** even now keeps under this rod.

<margin>p. 312.</margin>
Oh, how often one comforts another in such a state while feeling in his own soul the very thing for which he comforts others; and sometimes, when he finds that his own example might serve as comfort to others, he confesses and presents it to his sympathetic brother; otherwise, he carries his cross in silent patience and lets **GOD** alone be the witness of his struggle: so that it might not, if he says much of himself

All this shows how certain it is that such people stand in the state of grace, can please **GOD** therein, and can be saved.

We have, however, several objections to address. (1.) Such a tempted person says: "Alas! I must certainly have fallen from the grace of God, because He has given power over me to the evil enemy who stirs up such thoughts in <margin>p. 314.</margin> me; but he [the enemy] has no power over the children of God." Answer: Who says that the devil does not also have power over the children of God in some way at times? We certainly see this often enough, more than enough.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on spiritual trials (anfechtungen), moving from modern examples like Luther back to biblical precedents to prove that being harassed by the devil does not mean one is forsaken by God.</meta>

<page-num>224</page-num>
### The Third Sermon / ###

...enough. We have just seen how he spiritually assaulted <note>original: "angefochten." This refers to *Anfechtung*, a state of intense spiritual anxiety or trial where one feels attacked by doubt or the devil.</note> Job, Asaph, and Paul. In the Gospel history, we read that he often possessed pious people even in the body. If we do not wish to recall the example from the current Gospel <note>The sermon refers to the day's reading, Luke 11:14–28, concerning the casting out of a demon.</note>, the example of the daughter of the Canaanite woman is still in our memory; but primarily Mary Magdalene, the great benefactor of Christ (Luke 8:2), from whom the Lord had driven out seven devils.

The Gospel showed fourteen days ago (Matthew 4) how Satan dealt with Christ, tempting him for forty days—and finally in bodily form—and even leading him physically onto the pinnacle of the temple and a high mountain; and yet, regardless of this, He remained God’s beloved Son. Therefore, it does not yet revoke one’s <term>right as a child</term> <note>original: "Kinds-recht"; the theological status of being an adopted child of God and heir to salvation.</note> of God when Satan plagues someone with such thoughts in a spiritual manner. And we must not think because of this that he has thereby gained actual power and full authority to deal with us according to his will—

<margin>p. 315.</margin>
<margin>p. 316.</margin>

<meta>catchword: deal /</meta>

***

<page-num>225</page-num>
### Luke 11:14, 28. ###

-> K ❦ (2.) Says <-

—over us, for we saw eight days ago how the permission is limited and how **GOD** is always present, and what He does in the midst of it. Should Satan attempt anything beyond the granted permission, it is quite easy for [God] to pull him back in an instant. The fierce lion <note>A reference to 1 Peter 5:8, comparing the devil to a roaring lion seeking someone to devour.</note> may indeed play with us for a while and frighten us with his gaping maw; but as long as He is with us—without whom the devil may not even stir—he cannot harm us, and I defy him <note>original: "truz ihm"; an expression of bold confidence against an enemy.</note> to do us any true injury.

Paul says clearly in Ephesians 6 that we have to struggle against the ruler of the world who reigns in the darkness of this world, with the evil spirits under heaven: if we then have to fight with them, they must also be able to attack us, otherwise it would be no fight. Meanwhile, he tells his Ephesians not to place any doubt in their salvation because of this, but rather to be cautious and to strengthen themselves in **GOD**.

<margin>p. 317.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the pastoral response to "Anfechtung" (spiritual trials), specifically addressing the fear that sinful thoughts signify a state of damnation. It transitions from citing biblical examples of suffering to interpreting specific Pauline passages about the "flesh" and the "old Adam."</meta>

<page-num>226</page-num>
### The Third Sermon / ###

(2.) Suppose a person under spiritual trial <note>original: "angefochtener." This refers to someone experiencing *Anfechtung*, a crisis of faith or intense spiritual anxiety.</note> says: "Yet such thoughts are also from the flesh; now if we live according to the flesh, we must die eternal death," Romans 8, <margin>p. 318. 7.13.</margin> "the carnal mind is enmity against God," and 1 Corinthians 15:50, "flesh and blood cannot inherit the Kingdom of God." 

**Answer.** This proves nothing more than that the flesh indeed remains in the pious believers, and that this flesh, in and of itself, does not please **GOD**. This is what Paul long ago confessed in Romans 7, and yet added to it that, despite this, there is no more condemnation for those who believe, because they are in **Christ JESUS** and do not live according to the flesh. <margin>p. 319.</margin> Now, "living according to the flesh" does not mean being plagued by evil thoughts from the flesh while one resists them; rather, it means harboring them, taking pleasure in them, and following them. Thus, it would be asking too much of our current state of imperfection if we thought...

***

<page-num>227</page-num>
### Luke 11:14, 28. ###

-> K 7 <-
<meta>catchword: Chri</meta>

...we thought the <term>old Adam</term> <note>A theological term for the fallen, sinful human nature inherited from Adam, which persists in the believer alongside their new spiritual nature.</note> and the sinful flesh should be entirely dead and not stir: this honor belongs only to the state of that future perfection, for which—and for our translation into it—we must wait patiently.

(3.) One might say: <margin>p. 320.</margin> "Nevertheless, such thoughts are sins; but **GOD** the **LORD** hates sin, so a person stands therein in a state of sin, and is therefore under God’s hatred: if he dies thus, he dies in sin and must be damned." 

**Answer.** In this suggestion, the devil remains what he always is—a liar—even though he speaks the truest word of God. **GOD** hates sin: this is true. But it is not true—rather, the devil lies with such a pretense—when he says that **GOD** therefore hates all those in whom sin is found. It does not follow, then, that because **GOD** hates something in us, he therefore hates us ourselves. For **GOD**, who <margin>p. 321.</margin> hates sin, has nonetheless loved the world, which is full of sin:

<summary>This section distinguishes between the "flesh" (sinful nature) and "living according to the flesh," reassuring the reader that intrusive thoughts are not grounds for damnation if they are resisted. The author argues that God hates the sin within a person without hating the person themselves, exposing the devil's tactics of using scripture to drive believers to despair.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the pastoral response to spiritual anxiety, moving from the persistence of the "old Adam" to a crucial distinction between different types of sin. It addresses the believer's fear that their involuntary thoughts might lead to a total loss of faith and eventual damnation.</meta>

<page-num>228</page-num>
### The Third Sermon ###

Christ, who is an enemy to sin itself and came to eradicate it, nevertheless died for sinners; and if no one could be saved except for those who had no sin, the gate of heaven would be closed to all people. Here one should note the distinction between <term>sins of weakness</term> <note>Sins committed out of human frailty, impulse, or involuntary thoughts, rather than a settled choice.</note> and <term>intentional sins</term>. This distinction does not apply according to the Law, under which all sins make us guilty of eternal death.

<margin>p. 322.</margin>
Therefore, among the unregenerate <note>Those who have not experienced spiritual rebirth; in this context, those still outside of God's grace.</note>, all sins—among which there is nevertheless a distinction in severity—are particularly damnable. But, according to the <term>Gospel</term> <note>original: "Evangelio," the Latin dative form of the word for the "Good News" of the New Testament.</note>, when we have been made righteous through it and are no longer under the Law, then the Law's condemnation and judgment cease where Christ is. Thus, no sins are damnable to us anymore except those that take away our faith; but these are not the <term>sins of weakness</term> but the <term>sins of malice</term> <note>Deliberate, persistent sins committed with a wicked intent and a rejection of God's will.</note>, in which a person yields themselves to the service of sin, from which nonetheless

<meta>catchword: well</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>229</page-num>
### Luke 11:14, 28. ###
<margin>p. 323.</margin>
Christ has indeed redeemed us. Just as <term>sins of weakness</term>—among which these evil thoughts stirred up by Satan belong—do not abolish faith, so the person remains in God's grace despite them, as He does not cast them away, and that person dies in a state of salvation. All his sins, though damnable in their own nature, are not damnable to him, Romans 8:1.

(4.) Yes, you say: I am afraid of this; I know the weakness of my flesh, but the devil is cunning and strong; he will yet overpower me so that I yield to him and thus succumb, so that eventually sins of weakness could become sins of malice, by which I would be damned. 

**Answer.** Here we do not wish to make the person simply complacent <note>original: "sicher," meaning "secure" in a spiritual sense, often used to warn against false confidence or spiritual laziness.</note>, but rather say that he must certainly take heed of himself, and thus "work out his salvation with fear and trembling," Philippians 2:12. But for whomever God, through the providence of trial, allows such use to be hindered <note>meaning the person is so overwhelmed they cannot find the words to pray</note>, He requires that use from them just as little as He requires hearing from the deaf, or verbal praise and prayer from the mute.

<margin>p. 334.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on spiritual trials (Anfechtung), specifically addressing the concern that an afflicted person might lose the ability to pray or feel their faith. It bridges from the previous page's point that faith exists even when it cannot be felt.</meta>

<page-num>236, 237</page-num>
-> ### The Third Sermon ### <-
-> ### Luke 11:14, 28. ### <-

If the faithful heavenly Father should also deprive one of His children of understanding and senses for a time, so that they cannot pray, or even recall their own faith, they would nonetheless remain a dear child in that state in which their dear Father has placed them. But we must not say that such afflicted ones <term>German: "angefochtene"; refers to those undergoing *Anfechtung*, a profound spiritual crisis where one feels abandoned by God</term>—though they themselves may think and say so—do not pray in the midst of this unrest. For <margin>p. 335.</margin> we have recently shown that true prayer of the heart and of power becomes all the stronger the more unfit the afflicted person appears for prayer in human eyes.

Oh, how the anxious longing for God's grace—at a time when, in the soul's unrest, everything seems to be withdrawn from a person with which he might soar toward God—is a much more pleasant offering before God the Lord than many grand and ornate words. To be sure, when one is calm in their spirit, there is a great sweetness in being able to speak most intimately with one's God for a long time without
<meta>catchword: distracting</meta>

<column-break/>

<margin>p. 336.</margin> distracting thoughts <note>original: "frembde gedancken," literally "foreign thoughts" that interrupt meditation</note>, whereby the comfort in the heart increases more and more: so that it may rightly be regarded as a foretaste of that joy in eternal life, where we shall commune with God forever. But this <note>referring to the pleasant "sweetness" or emotional ease of prayer</note> is once again a "work" which is not strictly necessary for salvation; and God sometimes withdraws the ability for it so that we do not imagine too much of ourselves because of such devotion, or think that God is obligated to give us what we ask for its sake. On the contrary, <margin>p. 337.</margin> we recognize all the more that it is God’s pure grace when we ourselves have not even been able to present our prayerful longing to God the Lord, yet He, out of His love, allows our needs to be met. Then it is said that we **enter into life naked and bare**, when we see everything lost within ourselves which might otherwise have given us hope on our own part.
<meta>catchword: And</meta>

<summary>This passage reassures believers that even when they are too spiritually overwhelmed to pray with words, their "anxious longing" is a powerful prayer in God's eyes. The author argues that God sometimes withdraws the emotional sweetness of prayer to prevent spiritual pride and to highlight that salvation is by grace alone.</summary>

And we must therefore surrender ourselves purely and solely to God's mercy.
This is a deeper matter than we can fathom; indeed, no human tongues could adequately 
<margin>p. 338.</margin> describe it. In this matter, there remain many *inexpressible things* <note>original Greek: "ἀλάλητα" (*alalēta*), things that cannot be spoken</note>, just as Paul calls them the "inexpressible groanings" of the Holy Spirit in Romans 8:26. Let every afflicted person <term>German: "angefochtene"; refers to those undergoing *Anfechtung*, a profound spiritual crisis where one feels abandoned by God</term> take this frequently cited chapter to heart, not only to read it often but to devoutly weigh every word; there they will find more light than I can show them here.
Thus, according to the measure of divine grace granted to us, we have examined this intended subject together. Time no longer permits 
<margin>p. 339.</margin> drawing further exhortation and comfort from it. Whoever is earnest will know how to extract these for themselves from what has been said. Rough minds <note>referring to those who are spiritually unrefined or cynical</note> will hold this, like other things, in derision—but to their own harm.

> O holy, righteous GOD, who views all sins with abomination; just as You created us in perfect righteousness and holiness, You also require of us

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual trials (Anfechtung), transitioning from the advice of Jean Gerson to a lengthy passage from Johann Arndt's influential work, "True Christianity." It explores the psychological and spiritual state of a person who feels abandoned by God, explaining that even in the depths of despair and "unbelief," a hidden spark of faith remains.</meta>

# 252 Johann Arndt's / True Christianity. 253

<margin>p. 366.</margin> This **Church Father** <note>original: "Alt-Vatter." Referring here to Jean Gerson, the "Old Father" or venerable theologian mentioned on the previous page.</note> has taught and advised rightly and well; if we do not wish to put ourselves in danger, we must also act accordingly. For the more one indulges in these thoughts, the less one can rid oneself of them; instead, one is trapped as if in a labyrinth or a tangled building.

<margin>Johann Arndt's *True Christianity*, Book 2, Chapter 52.</margin>
In this distress, a person is plunged so deeply into **unbelief** <note>original: "unglauben." In this context, Arndt is describing a state of spiritual desertion where the believer can no longer "feel" their faith, leading to a sense of despair.</note> that he can no longer perceive his own faith. All the power of faith withdraws into a single point and into an **inexpressible sighing** <note>original: "unaussprechliches seuffzen." A reference to Romans 8:26, describing the Holy Spirit interceding for the believer when they are too overwhelmed to pray.</note>, in which faith is still hidden from him without his knowing it; and this hidden faith then feels to him like unbelief, and is his hell and his torment.
<margin>p. 367.</margin> In this hell, he cannot believe that God is gracious to him, and he says: "Ah, how gladly I would believe, if only God would give me the grace." Even the Scripture cannot comfort him until the storm has passed. There, God lets the

<column-break/>

man see his own **nothingness**, and what he is in himself, so that he might become as nothing in all his own powers. But nevertheless, God still allows Himself to be seen from afar, as if through those hidden, inexpressible sighs, and through this, the person is sustained. Even though a person in this anguish, torment, and hesitation is often impatient, and indeed even blasphemes, God does not hold it against him; for it happens against the person's own will, and it is his greatest **trial** <note>original: "probe." A test or refining process intended to strengthen the soul.</note>, through which God <margin>p. 36</margin> purifies him and sweeps away sin. Indeed, there are no greater saints and dearer children of God than those who endure such trials and chastisement, as we see in **Job**, **David**, and **Jeremiah**; for these learn faith in the true school. The delicate, **cross-fleeing** <note>original: "creuzflüchtigen." A term for those who want the comforts of religion but flee from the suffering or "cross" that comes with a devoted life.</note> saints think they want to learn faith on soft cushions without the cross.
Furthermore: Even if you might say, "Such thoughts that I must suffer are not from God, but from Satan." That is correct.

<summary>This page features a passage from Johann Arndt's "True Christianity" regarding the "hidden faith" that persists even when a person feels entirely abandoned by God. It argues that spiritual suffering and the feeling of "nothingness" are necessary for the soul's purification and true spiritual education.</summary>

# 254 Johann Arndt's / True Christianity 255

Satan can do nothing without God’s permission. Here, once again, take your Lord Jesus Christ as an example: God permitted Satan to tempt the Lord Christ. The words and fiery arrows <note>original: "feurige pfeil." A biblical metaphor from Ephesians 6:16 referring to the spiritual assaults and intrusive thoughts attributed to the devil.</note> that Satan spoke against Christ were certainly not from God, but from Satan; <margin>p. 369.</margin> yet God had nevertheless allowed and ordained it. And although Christ our Lord had to suffer and endure all of this, He was still nonetheless God’s dearest child, and Satan with his fiery arrows could not harm Him, even though he led Christ with him. Thus the Devil’s fiery arrows will not harm your salvation. Look at Job: how Satan, by God’s permission, plagued and terrified him, both outwardly and inwardly in his soul, so that he cursed the day of his birth; yet God was with him and preserved him. Therefore, he says in chapter 10, verse 13: **“Although you hide such things in your heart, yet I know that you**

<column-break/>

**think on it.”** <note>Job 10:13. In Arndt’s reading, Job recognizes that even in the midst of his suffering, God has a hidden purpose or "remembrance" of him.</note> Furthermore: <margin>p. 370.</margin> The withdrawal of comfort is more useful to us humans than all the glory of this world, although it becomes very difficult and feels long to our nature to lack and do without God for a time—indeed, not to feel or perceive Him at all. For this is a suffering above all suffering; yet in this trial by fire <note>original: "feuerprobe." A metaphor comparing spiritual suffering to the process of refining precious metals in a furnace to remove impurities.</note> one learns true humility, true repentance, and the disdain for the world, so that one turns away from all creatures <note>original: "Creaturen." In 17th-century theology, this refers to all "created things" (people, possessions, earthly pleasures) as opposed to the Creator.</note> and all transitory things, because one sees that in them there is no true comfort for the soul. And although the heart is struck with hellish anxiety and sadness, so that it cannot easily lift itself up to God, yet there is always a holy, hidden, mournful sighing and lamentation for God and His grace. From this we learn what <margin>p. 371.</margin> a high good God is, and that outside of Him there is no true, lasting comfort. This cannot be learned except in this school. And it is yet the highest thing we learn in this time

<summary>Johann Arndt explains that spiritual suffering and the feeling of God's absence are permitted by God to cultivate true humility and a detachment from worldly things. ## The 53rd Chapter.

In great spiritual trials <note>original: "anfechtungen." A central term in German Pietism referring to intense spiritual assaults, doubts, or "attacks" on the soul that test one's faith.</note>, one should observe and take to heart the following main grounds for comfort: 1. That these great, heavy trials, sad and melancholic thoughts, terrors of the soul, and anxiety of conscience do not happen to us without God's special counsel and gracious will, no matter how hard Satan sets upon a person. For God's word testifies in <term>Matthew 10:28</term> that Satan has no power over any creature, not even the <margin>p. 381.</margin> smallest, nor over a single hair or a blade of straw. Much less does he have power over a human being, unless it is allowed and permitted to him by God; as the history of the Gerasenes <note>original: "Gergesenische Historia." Refers to the account in Matthew 8 where Jesus permits demons to enter a herd of swine.</note>, <term>Matthew 8:32</term>, and the example of <term>Job, chapter 1:12</term>, testify. <meta>This page begins Chapter 54 of Johann Arndt's "True Christianity." # 284 Johann Arndt’s

## Chapter 54.

### Comfort against the internal, secret, and hidden temptations of the wretched Satan through evil, blasphemous, heart-plaguing, restless, and anxious thoughts.

<margin>p. 407.</margin>
->Luke 22:31<-

> **Behold, Satan has desired to have you, that he may sift you as wheat. But I have prayed for you, that your faith may not fail; and when you have turned back, strengthen your brothers.**

What a fierce and declared enemy of mankind Satan is, is testified not only by God’s word in many places—such as 1 Peter 5:8, **Be sober, etc.**; <note>The full verse is "Be sober, be vigilant; because your adversary the devil walks about like a roaring lion, seeking whom he may devour."</note> Ephesians 6:12; and Revelation 12:12, **Woe to you who dwell on the earth and on the sea, etc.**; and the history of Holy Job—but daily experience sets 

<meta>catchword: lets [continues as "sets"]</meta>

<column-break/>

# True Christianity. 285

<margin>p. 408.</margin>
it before everyone’s eyes, in so many terrible and pitiable examples and cases; yes, in so many various high and dangerous temptations <term>Anfechtungen: a specific Lutheran term for spiritual trials or "assaults" that test a believer's faith</term>, with which many a pious heart is anxious and tormented in such strange and various unheard-of ways that no one can guard himself against them. At one moment he tempts our faith, at another our calling, then he attacks our prayer, all of which is prefigured for us in the temptation of Christ (Matthew 4:3). From this we can infer that Satan does not leave off; he tempts all people, none excepted. For if he did not leave our Head, the **Lord Christ**, untempted, how should he leave any human being untempted? Therefore the **Lord** warns us in Matthew 26:41: **Watch and pray, that you do not fall into temptation.**

<margin>p. 409.</margin>
Among other temptations, however, Satan also has this: that he plagues a person with evil, blasphemous, shameful, and restless thoughts, whereby he makes the heart anxious and martyred...
