<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 285 regarding the nature of spiritual "Anfechtung" or assaults. Arndt has been describing the devil's tactics as psychological warfare, specifically the intrusion of blasphemous and agonizing thoughts.</meta>

# 286 Johann Arndt’s

...tortures and torments it so that it can never be happy; and such evil thoughts are so swift, so vehement, and so poisonous that one has not a moment’s peace from them. Therefore St. Paul calls them in Ephesians 6:16:
> **The fiery arrows of the wicked one**,
that is, just as an open enemy terrifies a city with fire: so Satan terrifies the heart with such hellish thoughts; and just as a most poisonous arrow makes a painful wound and unbearable aches: so the fiery arrows of the Devil create such anguish and secret pains of the soul that no person can describe them, <margin>p. 410.</margin> compared to which all bodily pains—indeed, all misfortune—are as nothing.

Against such secret, internal temptation <term>Anfechtung: a specific Lutheran term for spiritual trials or "assaults" that test a person's faith</term>, from which great soul-anguish arises, we will now hear several grounds of comfort from God’s word: 1. The LORD speaks these words:
> **Behold, Satan has desired you**,
very *passionately* <note>original: "pathetice", used here in the sense of pathos; expressing deep, heartfelt emotion</note>, that is, with a heartfelt compassion, and He thereby laments our misery

<column-break/>

# True Christianity. 287

compassionately. Therefore it is certain that such temptations are no signs of God’s wrath and displeasure toward a person—that God wishes to destroy us thereby and throw us into Satan's clutches <term>Rappuse: a 17th-century term for a "scramble" or "snatching," implying being left as helpless prey</term>—but rather it is a sharp rod of discipline through which the LORD God humbles a person, as the example of St. Peter testifies: that his presumption gave cause for his heavy temptation and fall. And St. Paul says in 2 Corinthians 12:7:
<margin>p. 411.</margin>
> **Lest I should be exalted above measure, there was given to me a thorn in the flesh, namely, the messenger of Satan, to buffet me with fists, for which I pleaded with the LORD three times that it might depart from me; but He said to me: My grace is sufficient for you, for my power is made perfect in weakness.**

This saying of St. Paul teaches us three things. First, that St. Paul, and all who are so tempted, are humbled through such blows of Satan. Secondly,

<summary>Arndt compares spiritual suffering to poisoned arrows and explains that these trials are not signs of God's rejection, but are intended to foster humility, as seen in the lives of the Apostles Peter and Paul.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual trials (Anfechtung) and the comforts (Trost) provided by Scripture. On the previous page, Arndt was explaining that trials are not signs of God's wrath but are a form of divine discipline intended to humble the believer, using the example of St. Peter's fall and restoration.</meta>

# 288 Johann Arndt’s

that it happens through God’s counsel.
Thirdly, that nevertheless such a person under spiritual assault <term>Anfechtung: a period of intense spiritual trial or doubt where a believer feels abandoned by God or attacked by the Devil</term> remains in God’s grace, even if the Devil assails him ever so greatly. As the <margin>p. 412.</margin> LORD speaks:
> **My grace is sufficient for you.** <note>2 Corinthians 12:9. Arndt applies this specifically to the inner torment of the soul.</note>

The LORD gives us the second comfort with these words:
> **Satan has desired you.**

Here we hear that the evil enemy indeed desires to shoot his fiery arrows in heaps against us, but he is not permitted; it is not always allowed to him. He must first ask God about it. And even when God allows him, He nevertheless sets a boundary for him and holds him like a **chained dog** <note>A common metaphor in Lutheran theology suggesting that the Devil’s power is strictly limited by God’s sovereignty</note>. Therefore St. Paul says in 1 Cor. 10:13:
> **God is faithful, who will not let you be tempted beyond your ability.**

There St. Paul says: GOD does not allow it to Satan, for **He is faithful.** This is evidenced by the example of Job, when Satan said: "Allow me." But God set a certain limit for him.

<column-break/>

# 289 True Christianity

The LORD gives us the third comfort <margin>p. 413.</margin> where He speaks:
> **But I have prayed for you, that your faith may not fail.**

How the LORD has prayed for us is evidenced in the 17th Chapter of St. John, where He commends His believers highly and dearly to His heavenly Father, that He may preserve them from the evil one, and that the Father may remain in them and they in Him, so that He might lose none of those whom God has given Him. This prayer will sustain all assailed and troubled hearts, so that they remain in Christ through faith, and Christ in them, and that through faith they may maintain the victory and overcome.

The beautiful verse in John 17 gives us the fourth comfort: how the LORD prayed for us and what He requested from God—namely, <margin>p. 414.</margin> that we may remain in Christ and Christ in us. Now St. John speaks in his 1st Epistle, chapter 4:
> **He who is in you is greater than he who is in the world.**

Within every Christian dwells...

<summary>Arndt provides four biblical "comforts" for those suffering from spiritual anxiety, emphasizing that the Devil's power is strictly limited by God and that Christ's own prayer protects the believer's faith.</summary>

<margin>p. 416.</margin>

Yes / you say / how shall I notice this? 1. By your **longing for faith**: For

<meta>This page transitions from Johann Arndt's "True Christianity" to a work by Sigismund Schererz. While Arndt focused on the comfort found in Christ's victory and the examples of the saints, Schererz moves into a specific, darker territory of "spiritual trial": the experience of intrusive, blasphemous thoughts that plague the believer against their will.</meta>

# **Sigismund Schererz**
**Flight from Melancholy, or Medicine for the Soul and Mirror of Spiritual Trial** <note>original: "Fuga Melanch. oder Seelen=Artzeney und Spiegel der geistlichen Anfechtung"</note> Book 2, Part 1, Meditation 6, page 34, and following pages.

*Concerning those who, against their will, experience abominable thoughts against God, His word, and the salvation of their own souls.* <note>original Latin: "De iis, qui abominabiles cogitationes contra Deum, verbum ejus, & animæ suæ salutem inviti sentiunt."</note>

<margin>p. 422.</margin>
**A report concerning those who must painfully feel and suffer in their hearts—against their will and intention—abominable and blasphemous thoughts against God, His Holy Word, and their own salvation.**

->Matthew 15.<-

> **Out of the heart come evil thoughts.**

Because the imagination and intent of the human heart is only evil from youth (Genesis 6 and 8), it is easy to see that without God’s inspiration we can harbor few good thoughts; for we are not capable of thinking anything good of ourselves, as if it came from ourselves.

<column-break/>

# Medicine for the Soul <note>original: "Seelen=Arzney"</note>
<sig>N 6</sig>
<meta>catchword: whether</meta>

Now, it is true that even pious and God-fearing hearts must suffer <margin>p. 423.</margin> when evil thoughts of anger, revenge, impatience, unchastity, and vanity rise up in their hearts (Matthew 15). They also labor without ceasing to overcome these through heartfelt prayer and the diligent struggle of the Spirit against the flesh.

But this is the worst of it: that they are sometimes assaulted—especially in a state of sorrow—with thoughts against God’s glory, truth, justice, mercy, and love. They dare not reveal these to any person, for they are far too terrifying.

Often they think, when they see that the godless flourish while the pious are oppressed, that God is unjust. When they consider that so many people are damned, they think: who knows if God does not wish it so? It often reaches the point that they become ill-disposed toward God and regard Him as their enemy, <margin>p. 424.</margin> especially where they are burdened with a great Cross <note>The "Cross" refers to a heavy burden of suffering or a difficult life circumstance seen as a spiritual test.</note>. They doubt...

<summary>This page introduces Sigismund Schererz’s pastoral advice for those suffering from "spiritual melancholy," specifically focusing on intrusive, blasphemous thoughts that occur against a believer's will. Schererz explains that such thoughts arise from a corrupted human nature and are intensified during times of deep suffering or social injustice.</summary>

# Sigismund Schererz

...whether it is true, what the preachers say from the pulpit. They often think: "Who knows if there is a resurrection of the flesh, and eternal life, and a hell? The preachers only say these things to keep people in fear," and such things. And even though they otherwise firmly believe everything in their Christianity and are convinced by God's word, they must still sometimes endure such spiritual storms of trial <term>trial: original German "Anfechtung," a term describing a profound crisis of faith where one feels attacked by doubt or the devil</term>.

Then a thought often occurs to them: "Oh, it is all for nothing—going to church, praying, fearing **God**, living honorably, doing right. **God** does not care about it. Who knows if you will even get into heaven?" <margin>p. 425.</margin> Often, thoughts follow after this that confuse a person’s body and soul, so that he becomes quite straying in his head and heart. He desires neither to eat nor to drink; he wishes to be separated from all people—from his spouse, children, and friends—and wishes that he were nothing, or that he might flutter away and in the wind
<meta>catchword: vanish-</meta>

vanish like smoke or a cloud. He does not desire heaven; he would rather that everything—heaven and earth, all people and creatures—would become nothing, so that he would no longer have to plague himself.

When we look at the holy **Job**, we find in various chapters that in his deep suffering, he had many strange and blasphemous thoughts. He even says in chapter 3: "Why did I not die from the mother's womb? Why was I placed upon the lap?" and in the 7th chapter, his soul wishes to be hanged. <margin>p. 426.</margin> But afterward, he is sorry for it and says in chapter 39 <note>Job 39:37 in the Luther Bible; modern translations usually place this at Job 40:4</note>: "Behold, I have been insignificant; <note>original: "leichtfertig," here meaning unworthy or of little account before God</note> what shall I answer? I will lay my hand upon my mouth. I have spoken once, therefore I will not answer; afterward, I will not do it anymore."

### 300 Sigismund Schererz

...had died in the womb; but there is no doubt that he was afterwards sorry a thousand times over that he had spoken such words.

And the holy teacher of the Church, *Bernard* <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a highly influential French abbot and mystic known for his intense devotion.</note>, was very much subject to such evil and wicked thoughts, as he himself confesses here and there in his books, especially in his book *On the Inner House* <note>original: "de interiori domo," a medieval treatise on spiritual life historically attributed to Bernard.</note>:
<margin>p. 427.</margin>
there he writes how many bitter thoughts he suffers in his heart, and how a great tumult of the same often arises within him, so that even all his good thoughts are scattered thereby; he knows no other way to drive these away than with patience, prayer, and sighing.

And just as the children of God—who often feel this wicked plague in their hearts during certain months, days, and hours—cannot exactly say or describe everything they think (and indeed rightly feel a loathing at revealing them), so I also cannot describe them all. Nor do I consider it advisable to record here all such devilish
<meta>catchword: intrusions</meta>

<column-break/>

### Medicine for the Soul. 301 <note>original: "Seelen-Artzney"</note>

intrusions <note>original: "einfäll," referring to sudden, intrusive thoughts that seem to "fall into" the mind against one's will.</note> which I have often heard from troubled hearts, even in the presence of others.

I say only in summary <note>original: "summa"</note> that in Christianity it is not the least of miseries, but rather a hard stumbling block, that pious hearts who intend to serve **GOD** holily according to His word must suffer so much unspeakable distress from their own intrusions—and that against all their will and purpose.
<margin>p. 428.</margin>
Their own thoughts terrify them so much (*Daniel 5*), and they become so troubled in their thoughts (*Daniel chapter 7*), that often even the strange and terrifying dreams—which are very common among such melancholics <term>melancholics: people suffering from "melancholy," a historical term for a state of deep despondency or depression, often viewed through a spiritual lens.</term>—cause them many heavy thoughts (*Sirach chapter 34*). And from this follows the heaviest suffering: that they further believe **GOD** will reject and condemn them from His presence because of such ghastly and loathsome thoughts; yes, they think there is no person in the whole world so plagued with such thoughts as they are.
<meta>catchword: Some [Manchen]</meta>

### Sigismund Schererz, Medicine for the Soul. ###

<margin>p. 429.</margin>
For many people, this inner misery ruins and embitters their entire life, all fear of God, all devotion in prayer, all love for God’s word, all beautiful trust in the dear God, and all hope for eternal joy and glory. Indeed, even if one fights and prays diligently against it, it often helps very little until such painful imaginings pass away of their own accord according to God’s will, finally granting the person a little peace and comfort.

But when a person dwells on such thoughts, then ten evil ideas grow out of just one. It is just like when one throws a stone into still water: the stone creates a circle, and from that one circle many more arise, until the ripples reach all the way to the shore. In the same way, when we fixate on our 

<margin>p. 430.</margin>
imaginations <note>original Latin: "imaginationibus"</note> regarding such fanciful <note>original: "visirlichen," an archaic term referring to things that are imagined, visualized, or fantastical.</note> thoughts, one circle and colorful, strange whim always grows larger than the next. Often Satan joins in, for he would gladly turn a physical cross <note>A "cross" here refers to physical suffering, illness, or external hardship.</note> into a spiritual

<column-break/>

spiritual soul-anxiety <term>soul-anxiety (Seelen-angst): a profound state of spiritual dread or existential panic regarding one's salvation.</term>. And just as boys in the thawing weather roll and wind a small ball of snow, turning it over and over until it becomes as large as a barrel—though it is later melted away by the sun—so too does Satan diligently roll our sorrowful thoughts together. He would love to make a great mountain out of them, but Christ, the Sun of Righteousness, finally melts it through his Holy Spirit so that it must vanish entirely.

Avianus <note>Likely Johannes Avianus (c. 1550–1617), a German theologian who wrote on music and spiritual comfort.</note> describes this spiritual trial <term>spiritual trial (Anfechtung): the experience of being tested by doubt, despair, or the feeling that God has abandoned you.</term> in this way: Such people answer with trembling and hesitation <margin>p. 431.</margin> because it sometimes seems to them as if God were not good <note>original: "fromm," which in this period meant "upright," "kind," or "righteous" rather than just "pious."</note>. They weep over this, often crying out and complaining that such thoughts strike them against their will, for they truly do not wish to think such things. Sometimes they may torment themselves powerfully and without ceasing over a conversation they had, in which there was actually no sin involved; or, if there was sin 

<summary>Schererz compares intrusive, distressing thoughts to ripples in water or a growing snowball, explaining how Satan uses them to create spiritual anxiety. He reassures the reader that Christ eventually melts these burdens and notes that sufferers often feel deep guilt over thoughts they cannot control.</summary>

### Instruction. ###

<margin>p. 433.</margin>
But when such burdensome thoughts enter the hearts of believers / they should first learn to recognize their corrupt nature / and conclude from it / that they will not earn heaven with their own holiness / as the hypocrites in the Papacy <note>A common Reformation-era critique of the Catholic Church, suggesting that people can earn salvation through their own "holy" deeds.</note> imagine / who do not consider this sickness of the human heart.

1. For if human thoughts are so evil / even against one's own will / what good could otherwise be accomplished by a person without God's government and guidance?

2. If such thoughts occur to you / and disturb you / then let them fall away again / and defend yourself against them as best you can / take up some work in your vocation <term>vocation (Beruff): one's daily work or station in life, which the Reformers believed was a person’s primary sphere of service to God.</term> / speak with pious and
<meta>catchword: you</meta>

# 306 Sigismund Schererz #

<margin>p. 434.</margin> to your trusting heart / then such thoughts will gradually disappear.

3. At such times, also take up beautiful little books of comfort <note>original: "trost-büchlein," small devotional books popular in the 17th century used for spiritual support during illness or trial.</note> / which are dear to you / and search within them with diligence / whether some small verse might be found therein / that might set your heart somewhat at peace / and free you from such unhealthy thoughts.

4. But should such thoughts persist for some time / to your greatest distress / and such evil ideas plague you relentlessly / then have patience / and do not prescribe any timeline to your dear GOD. Remember also / that he has certain reasons / why he does not immediately snatch you out of such inner suffering / whose fatherly love and deliverance you will certainly experience in due time / GOD well knows our weakness / that these are more *sufferings* <note>original Latin: "passiones"</note> than *actions of the soul* <note>original Latin: "actiones animæ"</note> / as the

### Augustine in *Questions on the Old and New Testament* ###

> Who is there even among those who fear God who is without sin? Since transgressions mingle in our thoughts, and we sometimes sin against our will. <note>original Latin: "Quis est etiam timens Deum sine peccato? cum in cogitationibus admisceantur delicta, & aliquoties peccemus inviti."</note>

->That is:<-

Who is there even among the God-fearing without misdeed? Since sins mingle even in our thoughts, and we often sin even against our own will.

### Dr. Heinrich Müller’s *School of the Cross, Penance, and Prayer*. Meditation 8. ###
#### p. 259. ####

The last arrow is the arrow of blasphemy; a fiery arrow, when Satan plagues a person with evil, blasphemous, shameful, and restless thoughts.

<page-num>312</page-num>
<header>Dr. Heinrich Müller’s</header>

[plagues] and thereby distresses the heart to such an extent, tormenting and agonizing it, that it can never again be joyful. Just as a poisoned arrow creates a painful wound and brings intolerable aches, so do these fiery arrows of the Devil create such angst and secret pains of the soul that no person can describe them; in comparison, all bodily pains—indeed, all worldly misfortune—are as nothing.

<margin>p. 442.</margin>
### Further, p. 281.

Against the wound that comes from the blasphemy-arrow, you will find a beautiful healing plaster <note>original: "heilpflaster," a common metaphor in 17th-century "soul-medicine" for a comforting scriptural truth</note> in the word of Christ: See, Satan has desired you; but I have prayed for you, that your faith may not fail. <note>A reference to Luke 22:31–32</note> The Savior speaks these words with great emotion. "See" signifies, as it were, that he has a heartfelt compassion for our misery. Thus, this temptation is not a sign of Divine wrath, nor is it intended for our destruction, but rather it is a sharp

<meta>catchword: sharp</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>313</page-num>
<header>School of the Cross.</header>

disciplinary rod <note>original: "zuchtrüthlein," literally a small rod or switch used for correction, implying a fatherly rather than a punitive discipline</note> through which GOD makes a person more humble, as Peter experienced after his fall. This temptation comes from GOD. For the Devil cannot release his arrow before GOD desires it and he has asked Him for permission. GOD allows it; GOD also sets the limit. <margin>p. 445.</margin> How can anything evil come from the highest good? Christ comforts the tempted when he says: *I have prayed for you, that your faith may not fail.* We find this prayer in the 17th chapter of John, where he highly and dearly commends his believers to his heavenly Father, that He may preserve them from the evil one, that He may remain in them and they in Him, so that he may lose none of those whom GOD has given him. This prayer will sustain all tempted and sorrowful hearts, so that they remain in Christ through faith, and Christ may obtain the victory in them through that same faith. If Christ is in me, then let Satan storm on; Christ will be his

<sig>Q 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: be his</meta>

<summary>This passage uses the "fiery arrows" metaphor to describe the intense mental suffering of intrusive thoughts and offers comfort by framing these trials as divine discipline allowed by God to foster humility, ultimately pointing to Christ's intercessory prayer as the believer's protection.</summary>

[will] well protect His house. If Christ is in me, then His Spirit is also in me, who will help my weakness and intercede for me before GOD with unspeakable sighs. <note>A reference to Romans 8:26, describing the Holy Spirit’s role in prayer when a believer is too overwhelmed to find words.</note> "Oh!" you say, "how can Christ be there where there is no faith?" Your faith, dear soul, shall not cease. You are indeed struggling against these blasphemous thoughts and feel them with pain; the power to fight comes from faith. But if faith does not cease, then victory must finally follow, so that you may boast with all the Saints: Praised be GOD, who has given us the victory through our LORD Jesus Christ.

# Stephanus Prætorius in his p. 445.
## XIII. Treatise, entitled:
### The Little Child Jesus in His Form. page 257.

For all the horrible blasphemies which a pious Christian feels in his heart against the Lord Christ and His Holy Spirit certainly come from the Devil; they are not natural, but supernatural, violent, and imprinted Devil-thoughts. St. Paul calls such thoughts that he has and feels in his heart: "What I hate, that I do" <note>original Latin: *Quod odi, hoc ago*, citing Romans 7:15</note>. Likewise, "I do not perform it, but the sin dwelling in me" <note>original Latin: *Non perpetro illud, sed inhabitans in me peccatum*, citing Romans 7:17</note>. The pious Gerson <note>Jean Gerson (1363–1429), a famous medieval theologian whose writings on the "troubled conscience" remained popular with Lutherans</note> calls it "passive sin" <note>original Latin: *peccatum passivum*</note>, a sin that one does not actually commit, but which one must suffer or endure. Indeed, it is the case with almost all the sins of the Saints that they are more "suffered" or "endured" sins than active sins. Under this Cross <note>original: "Creutz," here meaning a spiritual burden or trial one is forced to carry</note> one must have patience and study the Gospel of Christ.

<margin>p. 446.</margin>

<summary>This page offers spiritual comfort to those suffering from intrusive, blasphemous thoughts, classifying them as "passive sins" forced upon the soul by the Devil rather than acts of the will. Using the authority of St. Paul and Jean Gerson, it encourages the reader that even a "spark" of faith is sufficient for eventual victory.</summary>
<keywords>Heinrich Müller, Stephanus Prætorius, Jean Gerson, spiritual warfare, passive sin, blasphemy, faith, St. Paul, Lutheran devotion, temptation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual trials, specifically the intense internal conflict of a believer who feels they are losing their faith through "impatience" and blasphemous thoughts. It draws from the writings of Stephanus Prætorius (1536–1603), a Lutheran pastor known for his focus on "justification by faith" and the inner spiritual life.</meta>

<page-num>316, 317</page-num>
<header>Stephanus Prætorius / in Consolation for the Sick.</header>
<sig>D 4</sig>

# Again / in the 52nd Treatise /
->called:<-

## Consolation for the Sick.

->p. 1037 and following.<-

### Lament.

I am becoming impatient / and am beginning to secretly hate and blaspheme **God**.

### Answer:

<margin>p. 447.</margin> This is the common affliction of all His saints, especially the greatest among them / that in their impatience they secretly hate / curse and blaspheme **God** / so that they may indeed consider themselves equal to other sinners / and not elevate themselves above them. For it must come to this for all dear **friends of God** <note>original: "Gottes-freunden," a term likely referring to the pious or those in a close relationship with God, reminiscent of the medieval "Friends of God" movement but used here in a Lutheran context.</note> / that they recognize themselves not merely as ordinary sinners / but as excellent sinners <note>original: "excellente sünder," meaning "outstanding" or "extraordinary" sinners—a rhetorical device to emphasize that even the most devout feel they are the worst of all.</note> / that is / as the very coarsest of all / and say / "I have done that which no one else has done" / if they are to fall away entirely from themselves /

<column-break/>

fall away / and truly take hold of the foreign righteousness <note>A central Lutheran concept: "iustitia aliena" or "foreign righteousness," meaning the righteousness of Christ which is credited to the believer from the outside, rather than earned by their own works.</note> of the **Lord Jesus Christ** through true faith. For whoever still clings to their own yellow hairs <note>A metaphor for holding onto one's own perceived goodness or small, natural strengths instead of relying entirely on God.</note> / acts falsely before **God** / and does not grasp the little golden branch of the only true righteousness of **Jesus Christ** with an upright heart. Therefore **God** lets His dear saints fall deeply / and surrenders them to sins / if not to others / yet certainly <margin>p. 448.</margin> to impatience / and the blaspheming of His Divine Majesty / and lets the clever world run wild against such fine wisdom. He lets them reach the point where / when they think of their thoughts / words and deeds / they are as terrified / as if cold water were poured down their backs / or as if their hearts were pierced with a glowing iron rod. And if they were so foolish / that they always kept themselves in such mournful thoughts according to the teaching of the Papists <note>A common post-Reformation term for Roman Catholics.</note> and Sophists <note>Referring to Scholastic theologians whom the author views as relying on overly complex, legalistic logic rather than the Gospel.</note> / they would surely be the most miserable creatures on earth.

<summary>This passage addresses the spiritual despair of believers who feel they are blaspheming God, explaining that such trials serve to strip away self-righteousness. It emphasizes that true faith requires abandoning one's own merits to rely entirely on the "foreign righteousness" of Jesus Christ.</summary>

<keywords>Stephanus Prætorius, Consolation for the Sick, spiritual trials, blasphemy, impatience, foreign righteousness, justification, Lutheran theology, self-hatred, grace</keywords>

<page-num>318</page-num>
<header>Stephanus Prætorius</header>

St. Paul calls such miserable intrusions of the Devil "slaps in the face" <note>original: "maulſchellen," referring to the "buffeting" mentioned in 2 Corinthians 12:7.</note> and "fiery darts." The Papists <note>A common Reformation-era term for Roman Catholics.</note> call it penance.

<margin>p. 449.</margin>
I know a pious, learned man who makes the blasphemy of Christ and His Holy Spirit so coarse that it seems to him the Devil himself does not make it so coarse; he fears that **God** will one day cast him into the abyss of hell for it with a mighty, hard thunderclap. He also secretly curses his children, who are dearer to him than his own soul. He lamented this to me with wailing and tears, saying he does not know where all such hellish horrors come from.

But do not despair because of this, dear brother / for such blasphemies are not yours / but the wretched Devil's. They are not <term>human temptations <note>original: "tentationes humanæ"</note></term>, but <term>Satanic impressions <note>original: "impreſſiones Satanæ"</note></term>, that is / not human temptations / but the imaginings of Satan. By the permission of **God**, he carries out his play

---

<page-num>319</page-num>
<header>in the Consolation for the Sick.</header>

<margin>p. 450.</margin>
with you / and wants to see if you might grow pale at such a nature / and thereby despair of your salvation. These are not <term>actions <note>original: "actiones"</note></term> which you perform / but <term>passions <note>original: "paſſiones," meaning things one suffers or endures rather than things one does</note></term> which you suffer against all your will / as St. Paul says in Romans 7: The evil that I would not / that I do. Now if I do that I would not / it is no more I that do it / but sin that dwelleth in me. Yes, not only the sin / dear Paul / that dwells in you / but also the Devil / who is your sworn enemy / and cannot endure that your conscience has the peace of **God** / and for that reason looks for how he might find room in your heart / to shoot his arrows there. But **God** / who has otherwise completely taken you over / and has graciously possessed you / permits such things / so that <margin>p. 451.</margin> you do not exalt yourself because of your high enlightenment. Console yourself with this / dear Paul / and freely roll such sins of blaspheming **God** onto another <note>Meaning the Devil, who is the true author of the thoughts.</note>.

<sig>D 5</sig> <meta>catchword: And</meta>

<summary>Prætorius comforts a believer tormented by involuntary blasphemous thoughts, explaining that these are 'passions' suffered from the Devil rather than 'actions' of the believer's own will. He uses St. Paul’s struggle in Romans 7 to illustrate that such spiritual attacks are meant to prevent pride and should be blamed on Satan, not the sufferer.</summary>

And even if you / dear brother / committed such wretched sin from within yourself / without any incitement from the Devil / do not despair because of it / but be entirely of good courage / stand here like a Christian knight / and overcome such sin through the most steadfast faith in forgiveness. **Despise that blasphemy of yours against the Son of God, or even against the Holy Spirit, with a heroic and generous heart, if you have been changed by it, or rather preoccupied and burdened by it. For you are not a damnable Jew, but a justified Christian.** <note>original: "Heroico & generoso pectore despice blasphemiam istam tuam in filium Dei, vel etiam in Spiritum Sanctum, si ea mutatus, seu potius præoccupatus & oneratus fueris. Quoniam tu non es Judæus damnabilis, sed Christianus justificatus."</note> (That is: With a joyful and brave heart <margin>p. 452.</margin> despise this blasphemy of yours against the Son of God / or also against the Holy Spirit / if you have been changed / or rather previously occupied and burdened by it. For you are no damnable Jew <note>In the context of the 16th century, this refers to the theological polemic regarding those who have rejected the Gospel, contrasted with those "justified" by faith.</note> / but a justified Christian.) Here it is time / that your faith should raise itself up / and stand in

<column-break/>

<page-num>321</page-num>
<header>in the Consolation for the Sick.</header>
<sig>D 6</sig>

and **thrive and bloom** <note>original: "vige & flore"</note> (in the highest bloom) and burn like a new torch / yes, shine like the morning star and the bright Sun. Would you let yourself be turned away from the comfort of your salvation by a sin? You must indeed overcome the entire sea and flood of all your sins / if you wish to be a true Christian. A Christian derives his name from this: that he is a <term>man of wonders <note>original: "wundermann," referring to one who participates in the "wonder" of God's grace through faith.</note></term> / and can despise that which no one else can despise / namely the most gruesome sin / through faith in Jesus Christ. Know / dear <margin>p. 453.</margin> brother / know / against the multitude and fullness of your sins / that you have been redeemed from all your sins, not with perishable silver and gold / but with the precious blood of Jesus Christ. Know / that in the sea of **Holy Baptism** there have been drowned for you not only the lowly horsemen of Pharaoh / but also King Pharaoh himself / that is / the Devil himself / with his most prominent captains of all <term>mortal sins <note>original: "todt-sünden"</note></term> / even if they were utterly deadly.

<summary>Stephanus Prætorius encourages the believer to treat blasphemous thoughts with "heroic" disdain, asserting that through baptism, the Christian has already "drowned" the Devil and all sins. <meta>This page continues the pastoral counseling of Stephanus Prætorius, focusing on the assurance that Christ has already overcome the believer's most "coarse" or grievous sins. ### 322 Stephanus Prætorius / in the Consolation for the Sick. 323

You cannot make it so coarse <note>Meaning sinful or grievous.</note> / the **Lord Christ** has made it much greater. He has taken your coarse sins by the feet / and plunged them over their heads into the water / and thus completely drowned them. I mean here the water <margin>p. 454.</margin> of his blood / and the water of holy baptism. The coarse vices were the first / which had to be offered up <note>Or "dealt with first."</note> / so that you might have secure peace through your whole life. For this reason **Christ** appeared / and suffered much / so that you might be a **Prince of Peace** <note>Prætorius often applies Christ's titles to the believer to emphasize the "joyful exchange" where Christ takes the believer's sin and gives them His status.</note> / and dwell in houses of peace / and have eternal peace against sin / world / devil, and death / and even if you sometimes banish your pious **Lord Jesus** to hell and other strange places / and thereby try to see what happens when one blasphemes the **Holy Spirit** / he nevertheless knows your heart. He knows / how much you love him despite this / and how mightily you honor him. Yes / how much this nature grieves you / and how painful

<column-break/>

it is to you. He knows the roaring of your heart / and the gnashing of your teeth / and the hard struggle <margin>p. 455.</margin> against it.

If you wish to be free from such temptation / then pray / and say: **Lord Jesus** / lead me not into temptation / but deliver me from the Devil. As often as your heart or the Devil compels you to blaspheme the **Lord Christ** / just as often turn the page / and praise him with a loud voice / and with the very best words that you have ever learned / speak thus amidst the tumult of the struggle: **Lord Jesus** / you highly blessed **Son of God** / you **God** of all honors / to you be eternal praise and thanks given for all your benefits. All the angels in heaven / and all **creatures** on earth must praise and laud you. Through such **cannons** <term>Cartaunen: heavy siege cannons used in 16th-century warfare</term> the Devil is driven away / and you hold the field. But as has been said / let meanwhile the sin / the wrath, and the judgment of **God** be entirely dead within you / <margin>p. 456.</margin> but the righteousness and the grace

<summary>Stephanus Prætorius comforts the believer by explaining that Christ has already "drowned" their sins in His blood and baptism, and provides a strategy of using vocal praise as "cannons" to drive away demonic temptations.</summary>

# Johann Downame
## in *The Christian Warfare*.

...and the depiction of those sins which he has actually committed, or which he cannot overcome, so that he might, out of despair, cease and stop proceeding in sanctification; <margin>p. 459.</margin> then he <note>The "he" refers to the devil, often depicted in this literature as a master psychologist who uses a believer's own memory and conscience against them.</note> puts terrible blasphemies into his mind against God’s Majesty and disturbs him with exceedingly ungodly thoughts, so that he may keep him away from godly and holy meditations and make him despondent in all Christian exercises. And just as the King of Moab (2 Kings 3:27), when he could not withstand the power of Israel and could in no way make them stop fighting against him, showed them a loathsome and cruel spectacle by offering up his son and heir to the kingdom to his idol upon the walls—a cruel act which Israel could not bear to see, and they therefore withdrew—so too, when the Prince of Darkness is overcome in the spiritual struggle by our courageous and joyful course in godliness, he finally

<column-break/>

<margin>p. 460.</margin> undertakes this diabolical trick: that he presents truly hellish blasphemies to our thoughts, so that he may fill our souls with terror and fear, in case we might perhaps wish to stand still in our Christian course and turn back from the spiritual struggle to avoid such terrible thoughts and cruel ungodliness.

These hellish inspirations are of two kinds: 1. Those which seem to have some basis in corrupt reason; to bring these upon us, he often uses our sinful flesh as his evil instrument. And these sometimes arise from our great good fortune and prosperity, through which men are brought to the point that they not only forget and abandon the Lord, <margin>p. 461.</margin> but—just like a fat, spirited horse—kick at the Master who has fed it so well. At other times, they arise from heavy temptation <term>Anfechtung: a German term for a deep spiritual crisis or "assault" where one feels abandoned by God or attacked by the devil</term>, through which men are moved to murmur against God and over...

<summary>This page describes the devil's tactic of using intrusive, blasphemous thoughts to terrify believers into abandoning their faith, comparing this to the King of Moab's desperate human sacrifice. It categorizes these "hellish inspirations" as either arising from corrupt reasoning during times of prosperity or from despair during times of suffering.</summary>

That He does not see everything, or has no power and might to govern all things; that He is not 
<margin>p. 462.</margin>

<margin>p. 463.</margin> 
who has been a liar from the beginning and seeks my destruction through his lies—or the sinful imagination of my own corrupt flesh, which is blinded and seduced by the Devil, whose servant and slave it is. But where we cannot be freed from such temptations in this manner, let us take our refuge in the Lord through an earnest prayer, asking Him that He would...

<meta>This page continues the instructions for resisting "hellish blasphemies" or intrusive skeptical thoughts. <page-num>330</page-num>
->Johann Downame<-
<meta>catchword: we</meta>

enlighten our understanding with His Spirit, so that we may clearly distinguish His truth and the godless falsehood of the blasphemous inspiration of Satan; 
<margin>p. 464.</margin>
and that He would incline and govern our hard hearts, so that we subject our understanding to His truth and reject the damnable lies of Satan. And since the Lord does not accept the prayer of a vain and idle person, but rather of those who take up all useful means to achieve their desire, so we must work to fill our understanding with such knowledge by which we may become capable of encountering Satan’s deceit and cunning. To that end, we must diligently read and contemplate the **Holy Scripture**, which can instruct us toward salvation and is sufficiently suited to thwart all the cunning deception of Satan. And where he unashamedly wishes to call God’s truth into doubt, so must

<column-break/>

<page-num>331</page-num>
->in *The Christian Warfare*<-

we confirm our faith with full confidence in the truth

Yet even assuming / that such inspirations <note>The German *Eingebungen* refers to thoughts "breathed into" or suggested to the mind, often used for both divine inspiration and demonic temptation.</note> / should be attributed to our flesh <note>In 17th-century theology, "the flesh" does not just mean the physical body, but the corrupted part of human nature that remains prone to sin even after a person has turned to God.</note> / as well as to Satan / nevertheless a weak Christian has no reason to become fainthearted because of it : considering / that the flesh / that is / the corruption of nature and the sinful sin / is just as wicked and evil as the Devil himself / and they are both equal mortal enemies of the reborn man / who oppose and resist him in all things / whom he must also withstand. ### How may we assure ourselves / that we are resisting the blasphemies?

But here a weak Christian will soon ask / how he may be assured / that he has resisted those blasphemous temptations / and has not rather yielded to them / since his mind <note>original: "gemüth", referring to the soul's disposition or inner feeling.</note> is constantly attacked and confused by them? Answer / the outward 
<margin>p. 488.</margin> person resists them / when we do not yield to them in deed / with words or works / even if the senses are somewhat preoccupied. But internally we resist them / when we do not approve nor endorse them in the judgment of our understanding / nor receive them into our will / nor incline ourselves toward them with our desires / and take not the slightest pleasure or delight in them. Therefore when we reject the blasphemous suggestions in our understanding

### 4. Comfort / that these temptations are not the believer's sins / but their Cross.

Fourthly and lastly / such tempted persons should also know for their comfort / that the diabolical suggestions / with which they are so greatly tempted and plagued / are indeed harmful /

A theological distinction between the "evil of sin" (moral guilt) and the "evil of punishment" (suffering or hardship resulting from a fallen world).</note></term> (and not a <term>sin-evil<note>original: "sünden-übel".</note></term>) which the Lord, according to His infinite wisdom and goodness, turns to the benefit of His children. For when God’s elect live securely in their sins / and postpone the time of conversion / the Lord allows Satan to strike them with his threefold scourge / so that they may be moved / to take their refuge in Him through true repentance. He lets loose upon them the arrows of heavy temptations / so that He may wake them from the deep sleep of their sins / and drive them through extreme danger / to put on the <term>Armor of God<note>A reference to the "Full Armor of God" described in Ephesians 6:11-17, used as a metaphor for spiritual defenses.</note></term> / so that they may offer resistance / not only to these blasphemous suggestions / but also to all other temptations of the Devil / which draw or entice them toward some
<margin>p. 491.</margin>sin. Especially / when they have not made it a matter of conscience /

<meta>catchword: made /</meta>

<summary>This page explains that resisting blasphemous thoughts through inner hatred of the sin fulfills God's requirements, and that such temptations are a form of spiritual discipline or "cross" rather than a sin for the believer. Downam argues that God uses these trials to wake the "elect" from spiritual lethargy and drive them toward repentance and the use of spiritual armor.</summary>

### The means to be freed from those hellish blasphemies.

The best means that we can
use to free our mind from

love for the same.

### Our unfitness for holy exercises must not hold us back from them.

An afflicted Christian might object, saying that while this is certainly a good course of action for a person of calm thoughts, <margin>p. 502.</margin> it will nevertheless be of very little help to those who, in all <term>holy exercises<note>original: "heiligen übungen". <meta>The catchword "gen" refers to the continuation of the word "verunruhiget" (disturbed) on the next page.</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>361</page-num>
<header>in the Christian Warfare.</header>

are so disturbed by Satan's blasphemous promptings, and are so burdened and attacked in their minds, that they can neither pray, nor read, nor contemplate anything with right understanding and devotion. Instead, they are constantly and forever confused in these holy exercises by hellish promptings, to such an extent that their hearing, praying, and reading is not only unfruitful and useless, but even (as they fear) becomes a sin.

**Answer.** If they cannot perform their duty as they would like, they must do it as well as they are able. Indeed, the more they are hindered and disturbed, the more earnestness they must relentlessly apply in performing them. For these <margin>p. 503.</margin> exercises are our best spiritual weapons, by means of which we drive away the power of the enemy. Therefore, let no one allow Satan to persuade them to throw their weapons away in the hope of attaining peace,

### 4. We must not repeat the blasphemies in our minds.

4. If we wish to be freed from these blasphemous <term>promptings<note>original: "eingebungen". As soon as we sense them entering our minds, we must lift our hearts to God through prayer, testify to our abhorrence of them, and ask for the assistance of the Holy Spirit to strengthen and fortify us against them. Or where they have come upon us unexpectedly, we must drive them out immediately and not let them rest in our hearts. And after we have driven them out
